A geometrical description of untwisted 3d Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT with defects


Abstract
We give a simple, geometric and explicit construction of 3d untwisted Dijkgraaf-Witten theory with defects of all codimensions. It is given as a symmetric monoidal functor from a defect cobordism category into the category of finite-dimensional complex vector spaces. The objects of this category are oriented stratified surfaces and its morphisms are equivalence classes of stratified cobordisms, both labelled with higher categorical data. This TQFT is constructed in terms of geometric quantities such as fundamental groupoids and bundles and requires neither state sums on triangulations nor diagrammatic calculi for higher categories. It is obtained from a functor that assigns to each defect surface a representation of a gauge groupoid and to each defect cobordism a fibrant span of groupoids and an intertwiner between the groupoid representations at its boundary. It is constructed by homotopy theoretic methods and allows for an explicit computation of examples. In particular, we show how the 2d part of this defect TQFT gives a simple description of defects of all codimensions in Kitaev’s quantum double model.

1
Introduction

Context

Defects in (topological) quantum field theories and the associated categorical symmetries are of strong interest from a mathematical and from a mathematical physics perspective. They arise naturally as domain walls and field insertions in conformal field theories, see for instance Fröhlich, Fuchs, Runkel and Schweigert
[
FFRS
]
, Kapustin and Saulina
[
KS
]
and Fuchs, Schweigert, Wood and Yang
[
FSWY
]
.
Defects also feature
in models from condensed matter physics and topological quantum computing such as Levin-Wen models
[
LW
]
and Kitaev’s quantum double models
[
K
]
, which were identified as the 2d part of a topological quantum field theory (TQFT) of Turaev-Viro
[
TV
,
BW
]
type by Balsam and Kirillov
[
BK2
]
. The inclusion of defects into these models was achieved by Kitaev and Kong
[
KK
]
, who also identified their algebraic data.

Defects also play an essential role in modern approaches to topological symmetries in quantum field theory pioneered by Freed, Moore and Teleman
[
FMT
]
.
Similar defect structures are also considered in the framework of (stratified) factorisation homology
[
AFT
]
and were applied there by Keller and Müller
[
KM
]
to describe factorisation homology of surfaces with bundles and by Jordan, Le, Schrader and Shapiro
[
JLSS
]
to construct generalisations of quantum decorated character varieties.

In addition to these motivations from topology and mathematical physics defects in TQFTs are of interest from a representation theoretical perspective. They give new insights into representation theoretical and categorical concepts such as Frobenius-Schur indicators and Brauer-Picard group(oid)s of tensor categories, see for instance Farnsteiner and Schweigert
[
FS
]
, Priel
[
Pr
]
and Fuchs, Priel, Schweigert and Valentino
[
FPSV
]
.

In this article we focus on defects in 3d TQFTs.
A
d
-dimensional TQFT is a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathcal{Z}:\mathrm{Cob}_{d}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from a cobordism category
\mathrm{Cob}_{d}
, whose objects are closed
(d-1)
-manifolds and whose morphisms equivalence classes of cobordisms between them. In contrast, a defect TQFT involves manifolds and cobordisms with distinguished marked submanifolds that are labelled with higher categorical data.
They are encoded in a defect cobordism category
\mathrm{Cob}^{\mathrm{def}}_{d}
whose objects and morphisms are
stratified
(d-1)
-manifolds
and
stratified cobordisms
with higher categorical defect data. This makes them interesting also from a topological viewpoint. Whereas TQFTs give rise to manifold invariants, defect TQFTs describe
manifolds with embedded submanifolds of various dimensions, such as embedded surfaces or knots and links.

Defects in 3d TQFTs were investigated extensively. Defect lines in Turaev-Viro TQFTs
[
TV
,
BW
]
were first considered by Turaev and Virelizier
[
TVr
,
TVr2
]
and by Balsam and Kirillov
[
BK1
]
. Fuchs, Schweigert and Valentino showed in
[
FSV1
]
how topological defects in 3d TQFTs of Reshetikhin-Turaev
[
RT
]
type are related to the Witt group of modular fusion categories. In
[
FSV2
]
this was then specialised to Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT
[
DW
]
.
Line and surface defects in Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFTs were treated by Carqueville, Runkel and Schaumann in
[
CRS1
]
and domain walls in Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFTs by
Koppen, Mulevičius, Runkel and Schweigert in
[
KMRS
]
.

An approach to 3d defect TQFTs via diagrammatic calculi was initiated by Carqueville, Meusburger and Schaumann in
[
CMS
]
based on the Gray category diagrams developed by Barrett, Meusburger and Schaumann in
[
BMS
]
. These diagrams also feature in the orbifold approach to defect TQFTs by Carqueville, Runkel and Schaumann
[
CRS2
,
CRS3
]
and in
[
CMR+
]
.
State sum models for Turaev-Viro TQFTs
[
TV
,
BW
]
with defects of all codimensions were constructed by Meusburger in
[
M
]
and in more generality by Carqueville and Müller
[
CM
]
in the orbifold approach.

Motivation

Despite the number of results on defect TQFTs in the literature, their applications to gauge theory and to geometrical and topological questions are hindered by the fact that the algebraic data is quite involved. They are usually constructed with advanced categorical methods and rely heavily on diagrammatic calculi for higher categories. The resulting formulation is often rather abstract and implicit and involves auxiliary choices such as triangulations or cell decompositions.
This makes it difficult to interpret defect TQFTs in terms of
gauge theoretical or geometric concepts such as bundles, even in those cases where the underlying TQFT has a clear geometric interpretation.

Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT with defects

In this article we address this problem for the simplest example of a 3d defect TQFT of Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury type, namely untwisted Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT
[
DW
]
. The theory without
defects admits a direct geometrical construction in terms of
G
-bundles for finite groups
G
. In the language of Quinn’s finite total homotopy TQFT
[
Q
, Section 4]
, it describes the homotopy content of function spaces and groupoid cardinality
[
FMP1
,
FMP2
,
BHW
]
.
As it is directly related to Kitaev’s quantum double model
[
K
]
for the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[G]
and the associated Levin-Wen models, the associated defect TQFT can be applied to describe defects in these models.

The categorical data labelling defects in 3d Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT is largely understood and can be derived from the general defect data for Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury TQFTs by specialising to spherical fusion categories
\mathrm{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
of
G
-graded vector spaces with a 3-cocycle
\omega:G^{\times 3}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. This leads to the following labelling of the strata of defect surfaces and defect cobordisms with algebraic defect data

•

\mathrm{codim}
0-strata: finite groups (and 3-cocycles),

•

\mathrm{codim}
1-strata: finite sets with group actions (and 2-cocycles),

•

\mathrm{codim}
2-strata: (projective) representations of action groupoids for group actions,

•

\mathrm{codim}
3-strata: intertwiners between them.

In the following, we do not consider the most general data. We require all cocycles to be trivial, which implies that the strata of codimension 2 and 3 are simply labelled with representations of action groupoids and intertwiners between them.
We expect that our methods can be extended to the case with non-trivial cocycles, but this will be technically more involved, and we leave it for future work.

Main results

Our main result is the explicit construction of a Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT with defects - a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathcal{Z}:\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\mathrm{def}}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from a
cobordism category of stratified surfaces and stratified cobordisms with defect data into the category of vector spaces (Theorem
6.8
).

The construction does not use diagrammatic calculi for higher categories and does not require choices of triangulations or cell decompositions, although we work in the PL framework. It also
does not rely on previous results on Turaev-Viro TQFTs with defects such as the ones in
[
M
,
CM
]
and thus gives an independent construction of defect Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT. As in Morton
[
Mo
]
, which motivated some of our constructions, our defect TQFT
is formulated entirely in terms of elementary geometrical and topological
quantities such as bundles, fundamental groupoids, action groupoids for group actions on sets and representations of the latter.

While most of the results are obtained by homotopy theoretic methods, they can be expressed in a simple way and have a clear geometric interpretation. In particular, the formalism allows one to efficiently and straightforwardly compute the vector spaces associated to stratified surfaces and the linear maps assigned to cobordisms between them. This is rather difficult in other formalisms, and we illustrate this with a number of examples. Although choices of triangulations or cell decompositions are not required, we show that
making such choices results in a simple graph gauge theoretical formulation of the defect TQFT.
This allows in particular a direct application to quantum double models with defects.
We hope that our formalism can help to bridge a gap between more abstract approaches and concrete geometric applications.

Construction of the defect TQFT

The TQFT is constructed in two steps.
The first step involves only the defect data associated to strata of codimensions 0 and 1, referred to as
classical defect data
.
It constructs with this data for each stratified surface or cobordism
X
a gauge groupoid
\mathcal{A}^{X}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{X}
, an action groupoid with gauge configurations as objects and gauge transformations as morphisms.
We show (Theorem
6.1
) that this assignment defines a symmetric monoidal functor
C:\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\mathrm{def}}\to\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})
into the category of fibrant spans of essentially finite groupoids. The fibrancy of these spans has a direct gauge theoretical interpretation, namely that gauge transformations at the boundary extend to the bulk.

Concretely, these gauge groupoids are obtained as follows. We encode the combinatorics of the stratification of
X
in a graded graph
Q^{X}
, whose vertices represent the strata and whose edges describe local incidence of lower-dimensional strata to higher-dimensional ones. We also consider the category
\mathcal{Q}^{X}
freely generated by this graph.
The classical defect data then defines a functor
D^{X}:\mathcal{Q}^{X}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
that assigns to each stratum a groupoid constructed from the classical defect data.
By associating to each stratum
s
of
X
a topological space
\hat{s}

constructed from
s
,
we obtain another functor
T^{X}:\mathcal{Q}^{X}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
that describes the topology of the stratification and assigns to each stratum
s
the fundamental groupoid of
\hat{s}
. The gauge groupoid
\mathcal{A}^{X}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{X}
is then defined as the end of the functor
\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathrm{Grpd}}(T^{X},D^{X}):\mathcal{Q}^{Xop}\times\mathcal{Q}^{X}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
.

The second step in the construction also takes into account the defect data assigned to strata of codimensions 2 and 3, in the following called
quantum defect data
. With this data, we assign to each stratified surface
\Sigma
a representation
F_{\Sigma}:\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{\Sigma}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
of its action groupoid.

For each
stratified cobordism
\Sigma_{0}\xrightarrow{i_{0}}M\xleftarrow{i_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
with associated span
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma_{0}}\sslash G^{\Sigma_{0}}\xleftarrow{P_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{M}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{M}\xrightarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma_{1}}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{\Sigma_{1}}

the defect data associated to its 1-skeleton defines a natural transformation
\mu^{M}:F_{\Sigma_{0}}P_{0}\Rightarrow F_{\Sigma_{1}}P_{1}
.
We show (Proposition
6.7
) that these assignments define a symmetric monoidal functor
G:\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\mathrm{def}}\to\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
, where
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
is the
symmetric monoidal category whose objects are representations of essentially finite groupoids and whose morphisms are given by fibrant spans together with natural transformations.

By combining this functor with a functor
L:\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
that is analogous to the functors constructed by Haugseng
[
Ha
]
, Trova
[
Tr
]
and Schweigert and Woike
[
SW1
]
and given by the limit on the objects, we obtain the defect TQFT
\mathcal{Z}=LG:\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\mathrm{def}}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
in Theorem
6.8
.

Applications and examples

The first application of our results is in the context of Kitaev’s quantum double models
[
K
]
.
As shown in
[
BK2
]
, Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT for a finite group
G
is directly related to Kitaev’s quantum double
[
K
]
and the associated Levin-Wen models
[
LW
]
for the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[G]
. More specifically, this TQFT assigns to an oriented surface
\Sigma

the
ground state
or
protected space
of the quantum double model. It is also shown in
[
BK2
]
that codim 2 defects describe excitations in these models. The full defect data was identified in
[
KK
]
in the more general context of Levin-Wen models for unitary spherical fusion categories.

Our defect TQFT assigns to each stratified surface with defect data a vector space that can be viewed as generalised ground state of these models in the presence of defects and coincides with it, if the defect data is chosen to be trivial. It therefore provides a canonical definition of a generalised ground state. It also gives an efficient way to compute it that does not require choices of triangulations or cell-decompositions.
In Section
7.2
we compute this vector space explicitly for a number of examples and show how various defect constellations (excitations, domain walls, domain wall excitations) arise from our formalism via the specific choices of defect data and stratifications.

In Section
7.3
we treat a number of 3d examples. We derive a general formula for the number our defect TQFT assigns to a closed stratified 3-manifold with defect data and also
consider cobordisms with boundaries.
In all cases, the results are given in terms of simple geometrical quantities such as group homomorphisms from fundamental groups into finite groups, fixed points of group actions or representations of certain stabiliser groups. Choosing transparent defect data reduces the defect TQFT to the one without defects.

Structure of the article

Section
2
is purely algebraic. It contains the required background on groupoids in Section
2.1
, describes the categories
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})
and
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
in Section
2.2
and constructs the symmetric monoidal functor
L:\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
used in the definition of the defect TQFT in Section
2.3
.

In Section
3
we introduce the stratifications required in the definition of the defect cobordism category and their description by graded graphs. These stratifications are oriented stratifications of PL manifolds, subject to certain conditions on the
neighbourhoods of strata. We introduce a notion of local strata that describes the incidence of lower-dimensional strata to higher-dimensional ones. We then show how stratifications can be encoded in graded graphs.
Section
4
then introduces the defect data for untwisted Dijkgraaf-Witten theory and defines the associated defect cobordism category.

Section
5
introduces the gauge groupoids of stratified surfaces and cobordisms. In Section
5.1
we construct the functor
T^{X}:\mathcal{Q}^{X}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
that describes the fundamental groupoids of the PL manifolds associated to the strata of
X
. Section
5.2
introduces an auxiliary category whose objects describe the functors
D^{X},T^{X}:\mathcal{Q}^{X}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
associated to a stratification, and whose morphisms are induced by certain graph maps and natural transformations. In Section
5.3
we then define the gauge groupoid of a stratified surface or cobordism.
We give an explicit description of gauge configurations in terms of functors from fundamental groupoids assigned to strata into groupoids constructed from their defect data. Gauge transformations are natural transformations between such functors that respect the maps relating different strata.
We show that gauge configurations and gauge transformations are determined uniquely by the functors and natural transformations for strata of codimension
\leq 1
and
0
, respectively.

In Section
5.4
we give a simplified description of the gauge groupoid in terms of fundamental groupoids with basepoints.
In Section
5.5
we show that this can be simplified further for certain stratifications, such as triangulations or their duals. The theory then becomes a generalised graph gauge theory for a graph
\Gamma
that is the 1-skeleton of the Poincaré dual to the stratification. The gauge groupoid is then given as the action groupoid

\mathcal{A}\sslash\mathcal{G}=\left(\Pi_{e\in E_{\Gamma}}M_{e}\right)\sslash\left(\Pi_{v\in V_{\Gamma}}G_{v}\right)
,
where
M_{e}
is the set assigned to the codim 1-stratum dual to an edge
e\in E_{\Gamma}
and
G_{v}
the group assigned to the codim 0-stratum dual to a vertex
v\in V_{\Gamma}
.

Section
6
constructs the defect TQFT
\mathcal{Z}:\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\mathrm{def}}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
. In Section
6.1
we show that the gauge groupoids of stratified surfaces and cobordisms define a symmetric monoidal functor
C:\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\mathrm{def}}\to\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})
. After proving some auxiliary results in Section
6.2
, we then show in Section
6.3
that the full defect data of the theory promotes it to a symmetric monoidal functor
G:\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\mathrm{def}}\to\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
and that composition with the functor
L:\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from Section
2.3
yields a defect TQFT. We show that it reduces to the usual Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT in its formulation à la Quinn
[
Q
]
in the absence of defects.

Section
7
contains examples that illustrate the formalism. After some background on the defect data in Section
7.1
, we give a detailed description of the vector spaces assigned to stratified surfaces in Section
7.2
and relate them to defects in quantum double models. Section
7.3
contains examples of defect cobordisms. The appendix contains some purely categorical results that are used in the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

The reader mainly interested in applications and examples may focus on Sections
5.4
,
5.5
and
7
.

2
Spans of groupoids and groupoid representations

2.1
Groupoids and groupoid representations

Recall that the category
\mathrm{Grpd}
of groupoids is complete and cocomplete, that it is a cartesian monoidal category and that it
is cartesian closed
[
Ke
, Section 1.1]
. The exponential of a groupoid
\mathcal{H}
by a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is the groupoid
\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{G}}=\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
of functors from
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathcal{H}
and natural transformations between them. Given groupoids
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
, we have a canonical isomorphism of groupoids, natural in
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
,

\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})\cong\mathrm{GRPd}\big(\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{K}^{\mathcal{H}}}\big).

(1)

We have a functor
\mathrm{Grpd}^{op}\times\mathrm{Grpd}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
that sends a pair
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
to
\mathcal{K}^{\mathcal{H}}
. For a functor
F:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
of groupoids we denote by
F^{*}:=\mathrm{GRPd}(F,\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{K}}):\mathcal{K}^{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{K}^{\mathcal{G}}
the functor that precomposes with
F
. More generally,
we have a composition functor
\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})\times\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})\to\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})

that corresponds to a functor

M:\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})\to\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})^{\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})}

under the isomorphism in (
1
). This functor
M
sends a functor
F:\mathcal{G}\to H
to
M(F)=F^{*}
and a natural transformation
\eta:F_{1}\Rightarrow F_{2}
to the natural transformation
\eta^{*}:=M(\eta):F_{1}^{*}\Rightarrow F_{2}^{*}
with components
\eta^{*}_{f}=f\eta:fF_{1}\Rightarrow fF_{2}
for objects
f:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
in
\mathrm{GRPd}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
.

The category
\mathrm{Grpd}
has a model category structure. A functor
F:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{B}
is a
fibration
if for every morphism
h:B^{\prime}\to F(E)
in
\mathcal{B}
with
E\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{E}
, there is a morphism
g:E^{\prime}\to E
with
F(g)=h
. It is a
cofibration
if it is injective on the objects. It is a
weak equivalence
if it is an equivalence of groupoids, see for instance
[
H
]
.

We denote by
\bullet
the terminal and by
\emptyset
the initial object in the category
\mathrm{Grpd}
. The
interval groupoid
is the groupoid
I
with objects 0,1 and unique morphism
d\colon 0\to 1
.
The
arrow groupoid

\mathcal{G}^{I}
for a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
has as objects morphisms in
\mathcal{G}
. Morphisms in
\mathcal{G}^{I}
from
f:G_{0}\to G_{1}
to

f^{\prime}:G^{\prime}_{0}\to G^{\prime}_{1}
are pairs
(f_{0},f_{1})
of morphisms
f_{0}:G_{0}\to G^{\prime}_{0}
and
f_{1}:G_{1}\to G^{\prime}_{1}
with

f^{\prime}\circ f_{0}=f_{1}\circ f
.
There are functors
p_{0},p_{1}:\mathcal{G}^{I}\to\mathcal{G}
given by
p_{i}(f)=G_{i}
and
p_{i}(f_{0},f_{1})=f_{i}
.
The groupoid morphism
\langle p_{0},p_{1}\rangle\colon\mathcal{G}^{I}\to\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}
induced by the universal property of the product is a fibration of groupoids.

For each groupoid
\mathcal{G}
there are functors
\iota^{0}=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}},0),\iota^{1}=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}},1):\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}\times I
and a natural transformation

\iota^{d}=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}},d):\iota^{0}\Rightarrow\iota^{1}
.
Functors
F:\mathcal{G}\times I\to\mathcal{H}
correspond to natural transformations
F^{d}:F^{0}\Rightarrow F^{1}
between functors
F^{0}=F\iota^{0},F^{1}=F\iota^{1}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
.

For a groupoid
\mathcal{B}
the slice category
\mathrm{Grpd}_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathrm{Grpd}/\mathcal{B}
of
groupoids over
\mathcal{B}
has as objects morphisms
p:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}
in
\mathrm{Grpd}
.
Morphisms from
p:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}
to
p^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}
are functors
f:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
with
p^{\prime}f=p
, called
functors over
\mathcal{B}
and denoted
f:p\to_{\mathcal{B}}p^{\prime}
. A
natural transformation
\eta:f\Rightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}f^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{B}
from
f:p\to_{\mathcal{B}}p^{\prime}
to
f^{\prime}:p\to_{\mathcal{B}}p^{\prime}
is a natural transformation
\eta:f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}
with
p^{\prime}(\eta_{G})=\mathrm{id}_{p(G)}
for all
G\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}
.

For any groupoid
\mathcal{B}
the category
\mathrm{Grpd}_{\mathcal{B}}
has an induced model category structure, where a functor over
\mathcal{B}
is a fibration, cofibration or weak equivalence if and only if the underlying
functor between
groupoids is, see
[
Hir
, Theorem 7.6.5]
. In particular, an object
p:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}
of
\mathrm{Grpd}_{\mathcal{B}}
is fibrant if and only if the functor
p:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}
is a fibration, and any groupoid over
\mathcal{B}
is cofibrant in
\mathrm{Grpd}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
p:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}
and
p^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}
be fibrations in
\mathrm{Grpd}
. Then any functor
f:p\to_{\mathcal{B}}p^{\prime}
of groupoids over
\mathcal{B}
that is an equivalence of groupoids is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Grpd}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

By the Whitehead lemma
[
Hir
, Theorem 7.5.10]
the functor
f:p\to_{\mathcal{B}}p^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{B}

is a homotopy equivalence of groupoids over
\mathcal{B}
. Now observe that for any object
p:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}
of
\mathrm{Grpd}_{\mathcal{B}}
, a cylinder object is the groupoid
\mathcal{G}\times I
, together with the functor

p\pi_{1}:\mathcal{G}\times I\to\mathcal{B}
, where
\pi_{1}:\mathcal{G}\times I\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection. This allows one to pass from a homotopy equivalence in
\mathrm{Grpd}_{\mathcal{B}}
to an equivalence of groupoids over
\mathcal{B}
.
∎

We denote by
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces over
\mathbb{C}
. A functor
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is called a
representation
of
\mathcal{G}
and a natural transformation
\eta:\rho\Rightarrow\rho^{\prime}
between such functors an
intertwiner
.
For a representation
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
we denote by
\rho^{*}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
the
dual representation
given by
\rho^{*}(G)=\rho(G)^{*}
for
G\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}
and
\rho^{*}(f)=\rho(f^{-1})^{*}:\rho^{*}(G)\to\rho^{*}(G^{\prime})
on the morphisms
f:G\to G^{\prime}
. The
trivial representation
of a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is given by the constant functor
\mathbb{C}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
that assigns to each object the vector space
\mathbb{C}
and to each morphism the identity map
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

For any groupoid
\mathcal{G}
the category
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathcal{G}}
of representations of
\mathcal{G}
and intertwiners between them inherits a symmetric monoidal structure from
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
, with the pointwise tensor product. The dual object of a representation
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the dual representation
\rho^{*}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
and the evaluation and coevaluation are induced
by the evaluation and coevaluation of
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The category
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathcal{G}}
also inherits a canonical pivotal structure from
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
and thus becomes a spherical category.

The
action groupoid
for an action
\rhd:G\times M\to M
of a group
G
on a set
M
is denoted
M\sslash G
and has

•

as objects elements of the set
M
,

•

as morphisms from
m\in M
to
m^{\prime}\in M
group elements
g\in G
with
g\rhd m=m^{\prime}
.

For a

G\times H^{op}
-set
M
we denote by
\rhd:(G\times H)\times M\to M
,
(g,h)\rhd m=g\rhd m\lhd h^{-1}

the associated left action of
G\times H
on
M
with action groupoid
M\sslash G\times H
.

Functors between action groupoids can be constructed from group homomorphisms and maps between sets that satisfy an equivariance condition with respect to the group actions and group homomorphisms. Such functors can be described more formally in terms of the following category.

Definition 2.2
.

The category
\mathrm{GrMod}
has

•

as objects pairs
(G,M)
of a group
G
and a
G
-set
M
,

•

as morphisms
(\phi,f):(G,M)\to(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
pairs of a group homomorphism
\phi:G\to G^{\prime}
and a map
f:M\to M^{\prime}
such that

f(g\rhd m)=\phi(g)\rhd f(m)
for all
g\in G
and
m\in M
.

Lemma 2.3
.

There is a functor
\mathrm{Act}:\mathrm{GrMod}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
that sends

•

an object
(G,M)
to the action groupoid
M\sslash G
,

•

a morphism
(\phi,f):(G,M)\to(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
to the functor
F:M\sslash G\to M^{\prime}\sslash G^{\prime}
given by
f:M\to M^{\prime}
on the objects and by
\phi:G\to G^{\prime}
on the morphisms.

2.2
Fibrant spans of groupoids and spans of groupoid representations

In this section we assemble the background and some relevant facts on spans of groupoids and groupoid representations. Recall first the definition of a span in a category
\mathcal{C}
and of the associated category of spans in a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category.
A
span
in
\mathcal{C}
from
X_{1}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{C}
to
X_{2}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{C}
is a diagram
X_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}A\xrightarrow{P_{2}}X_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
. Two spans
X_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}A\xrightarrow{P_{2}}X_{2}
and
X_{1}\xleftarrow{P^{\prime}_{1}}A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}_{2}}X_{2}
from
X_{1}
to
X_{2}
are called equivalent, and we write

(X_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}A\xrightarrow{P_{2}}X_{2})\sim(X_{1}\xleftarrow{P^{\prime}_{1}}A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}_{2}}X_{2}),

if there is an isomorphism
f:A\to A^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.77777pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 35.18056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.68056pt\raise-11.4489pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.93056pt\raise-11.4489pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.93056pt\raise-14.93552pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 74.08334pt\raise-8.66147pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-22.8978pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 33.77777pt\raise-22.8978pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
\mathcal{C}
has pullbacks then there is a category
\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{C})
with the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and with equivalence classes of spans from
X_{0}
to
X_{1}
as morphisms from
X_{0}
to
X_{1}
. The composition is via pullback

[X_{2}\xleftarrow{Q_{1}}B\xrightarrow{Q_{2}}X_{3}]\circ[X_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}A\xrightarrow{P_{2}}X_{2}]=[X_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}T_{1}}A\times_{X_{2}}B\xrightarrow{Q_{2}T_{2}}X_{3}],

where
T_{1}:A\times_{X_{2}}{B}\to A
and

T_{2}:A\times_{X_{2}}{B}\to B

denote pullback projections.
The identity on an object is given by the equivalence class of the identity span:

\mathrm{id}_{A}=[A\xleftarrow{\mathrm{id}_{A}}A\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{A}}A].

Dually, we can define cospans in a category
\mathcal{C}
and the category
\mathrm{cospan}(\mathcal{C})
, if
\mathcal{C}
is a category with pushouts.

In the following, it will be essential
the spans under consideration
are
fibrant
. The category
\Lambda=(0\leftarrow 1\rightarrow 0^{\prime})
is an inverse category in the sense of
[
Hv
, §5]
. Consequently, the category
\mathrm{Grpd}^{\Lambda}
, whose objects are spans of groupoids,
can be provided with the
injective

model category structure, using the nomenclature of
loc cit
. A fibrant span is a fibrant object with respect to this model category structure. More concretely, this is stated as follows.

Definition 2.6
.

A
span
of groupoids
\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{F_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{F_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}
is called
fibrant
, if the
induced functor
\langle F_{1},F_{2}\rangle:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
is a fibration.

We will only consider spans of essentially finite groupoids. A groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is called
essentially finite
, if it is equivalent to a
finite
groupoid, a groupoid with finitely many objects and morphisms.
In an essentially finite groupoid there are only finitely many isomorphism classes of objects and all Hom-sets are finite.

Fibrant spans of essentially finite groupoids also
form a category, which we will see later is symmetric monoidal.
However, its identity morphisms are not given by the identity spans that define the identity morphisms in

\mathrm{span}(\mathrm{Grpd})
. Those spans are in general not fibrant.
Instead, the identity morphism on a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is defined by the arrow groupoid

\mathcal{G}^{I}
and its projection functors
p_{0},p_{1}:\mathcal{G}^{I}\to\mathcal{G}
from Section
2.1
.

Lemma 2.7
.

We have a symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{span}^{\rm fib}(\mathrm{Grpd})
, which is given as follows:

•

Objects are essentially finite groupoids
\mathcal{G}
.

•

Morphisms from
\mathcal{G}_{1}
to
\mathcal{G}_{2}
are equivalence classes of fibrant spans
\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}
of essentially finite groupoids.

Two spans
are equivalent,

(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2})\sim_{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P^{\prime}_{1}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2})
,
if there are functors
F:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
with
P^{\prime}_{i}F=P_{i}
and
P_{i}F^{\prime}=P^{\prime}_{i}
that form an equivalence over
\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
: there are natural isomorphisms
\eta:F^{\prime}F\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\eta^{\prime}:FF^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
with
P_{i}\eta=\mathrm{id}_{P_{i}}
and
P^{\prime}_{i}\eta^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}_{P^{\prime}_{i}}
.

•

Composition of morphisms is by pullback, where
T_{1}:\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{H}
and
T_{2}:\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}
denote pullback projections:

[\mathcal{G}_{2}\xleftarrow{Q_{1}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{Q_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{3}]\circ[\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{H}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}]=[\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}T_{1}}\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{Q_{2}T_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{3}].

•

The identity morphism on
\mathcal{G}
is
[\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{I}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G}]
.

•

The symmetric monoidal structure is given by the product of groupoids. The associativity and unit constraints and the symmetric braiding of
\mathrm{span}^{\rm fib}(\mathrm{Grpd})
are obtained from those of
\mathrm{Grpd}
, as an isomorphism
f:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of groupoids defines a fibrant span

[\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{I}\xrightarrow{f\,p_{1}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}].

Proof.

This is proven in
[
To
, Section 3.2]
, in the dual context of cofibrant spans of topological spaces and in
[
FMP2
, Section 3.2]
in the context of homotopy finite spans of homotopy finite spaces. The argument generalises
to the context of groupoids via Lemma
2.1
. Note that the fibrancy condition on spans is essential to ensure that the composition of fibrant spans of essentially finite groupoids is a fibrant span of essentially finite groupoids. It is also required to ensure that
[\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{I}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G}]
indeed acts as an identity and that the associativity, unit constraints and braiding are natural isomorphisms.
∎

We now consider a category that is closely related to
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})
, where the objects are representations of groupoids and morphisms are given by fibrant spans of groupoids, together with certain natural transformations. Identity morphism on a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
are again defined by the arrow groupoid

\mathcal{G}^{I}
and its projection functors
p_{0},p_{1}:\mathcal{G}^{I}\to\mathcal{G}
.
For this, note that any representation
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
defines functors
\rho p_{0},\rho p_{1}:\mathcal{G}^{I}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}

and a natural transformation

\sigma^{\rho}:\rho p_{0}\Rightarrow\rho p_{1}
with components
\sigma^{\rho}_{f}=\rho(f)
for all objects
f
of
\mathcal{G}^{I}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

There is a symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
, given as follows:

•

Objects are pairs
(\mathcal{G},\rho)
of an essentially finite groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and a functor
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

Morphisms from
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\rho_{1})
to
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\rho_{2})
are equivalence classes of pairs
(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma)
of a fibrant span
\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}
of essentially finite groupoids and a natural transformation
\sigma:\rho_{1}P_{1}\Rightarrow\rho_{2}P_{2}
. Two such pairs are equivalent

(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma)\sim_{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P^{\prime}_{1}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma^{\prime}),

if there are functors
F:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
with
P^{\prime}_{i}F=P_{i}
,
P_{i}F^{\prime}_{i}=P^{\prime}_{i}
, that form an equivalence over
\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
and satisfy
\sigma^{\prime}F=\sigma
and
\sigma F^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}
.

•

Composition of morphisms given by pullback and composition of natural transformations

[(\mathcal{G}_{2}\xleftarrow{Q_{1}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{Q_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{3},\tau)]\circ[(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{H}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma)]=\big[\big(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}T_{1}}\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{Q_{2}T_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{3},(\tau T_{2})\circ(\sigma T_{1})\big)\big].

•

The identity morphism on
(\mathcal{G},\rho)
is
[(\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{I}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G},\sigma^{\rho})]
with

\sigma^{\rho}_{f}=\rho(f)
for all morphisms
f
in
\mathcal{G}
.

•

The symmetric monoidal structure is given by the product of groupoids and the tensor product in
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

That the composition descends to the quotient and is associative is proven with no difficulty. So we will focus on the unit axiom, which is not straightforward.

1. Let
\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{q_{1}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{q_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}
be a fibrant span of essentially finite groupoids,
\rho_{1}:\mathcal{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\rho_{2}:\mathcal{G}_{2}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
representations and
\sigma:\rho_{1}q_{1}\Rightarrow\rho_{2}q_{2}

a natural transformation. We show that

(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{q_{1}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{q_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma)\sim_{\mathrm{fib}}{(\mathcal{G}_{2}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma^{\rho_{2}})}\circ(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{q_{1}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{q_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma).

For this we consider the following diagram, which commutes up to the three natural transformations shown and where the bottom quadrilateral is a pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\sigma}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{q_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{q_{2}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\sigma}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\rho_{2}}}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}}

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{2}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}}

The functor
F
is induced via the universal property of the pullback by the identity functor
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{K}}:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}
and the functor
Iq_{2}:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}
, where
I:\mathcal{G}_{2}\to\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}
is the inclusion that sends objects of
\mathcal{G}_{2}
to constant functors and morphisms to constant natural transformations. The commuting diagram implies

(\sigma^{\rho_{2}}T_{2}F)\circ(\sigma T_{1}F)=\sigma
.

2. By Lemma
2.1
,
there exists a functor
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{K}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}\to\mathcal{K}
that also makes the diagram commute, up to the shown natural transformations, and such that there are natural isomorphisms over
\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}

F^{\prime}F\xRightarrow[\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}]{\eta}{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{K}}}\qquad\textrm{
and }\qquad FF^{\prime}\xRightarrow[\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}]{\eta^{\prime}}{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{K}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}}}.

To complete the proof, we need an explicit expression for such a functor
F^{\prime}
, obtained as follows.
For each object

(k,f)
of
\mathcal{K}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}
, where
k\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{K}
and
f:q_{2}(k)\to y
is a morphism in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
, we fix a lift
\hat{f}_{k}:k\to k_{f}
of
(1_{q_{1}(k)},f)
with respect to the fibration
\langle q_{1},q_{2}\rangle:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
. We choose the lifts such that
(\hat{1}_{x})_{k}=1_{k}
, whenever
{q_{2}}(k)=x
.
We then define

F^{\prime}\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.15245pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.76042pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.15245pt\raise-15.86555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.7133pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 31.03357pt\raise-30.41168pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{y^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right).

The inverses of the lifts
\hat{f}_{k}:k\to k_{f}
then define a natural transformation
\eta^{\prime}:FF^{\prime}\xRightarrow[\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}]{}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{K}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}}
.
Therefore,
F
and
F^{\prime}
define an equivalence over
\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
.

3. The natural transformation for the composite span is
\sigma^{\prime}=({\sigma^{\rho_{2}}}T_{2})\circ(\sigma T_{1}):\rho_{1}q_{1}T_{1}\Rightarrow\rho_{2}p_{1}T_{2}
. It remains to show that
\sigma F^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime}F=\sigma
. From 1. we have
\sigma^{\prime}F=(\sigma^{\rho_{2}}T_{2}F)\circ(\sigma T_{1}F)=\sigma
. Using the naturality of
\sigma
and that
\rho_{1}q_{1}(\hat{f}_{k})=1_{\rho_{1}q_{1}(k)}
, we obtain for all objects
(k,f)
in
\mathcal{K}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}^{I}

\displaystyle(\sigma F^{\prime})_{(k,f)}=\sigma_{k_{f}}=\rho_{2}q_{2}(\hat{f}_{k})\circ\sigma_{k}\circ\rho_{1}q_{1}(\hat{f}_{k})^{-1}=({\sigma^{\rho_{2}}}T_{2})_{(k,f)}\circ\sigma_{k}=({\sigma^{\rho_{2}}}T_{2})_{(k,f)}\circ(\sigma T_{1})_{(k,f)}=\sigma^{\prime}_{(k,f)}.

∎

Remark 2.9
.

The symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})
from Lemma
2.7
can be viewed as a subcategory of
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
, whose objects are pairs
(\mathcal{G},\mathbb{C}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}})
of a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and the constant functor
\mathbb{C}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
and whose morphisms are equipped with identity natural transformations.

In the following, it will sometimes be more practical to use different representatives of the identity morphisms in
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})
and in
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
.
Those are obtained from the functor
\Pi_{1}:\mathrm{Top}\to\mathrm{Grpd}
that assigns to a topological space its fundamental groupoid and to a continuous map the induced functor between fundamental groupoids. The inclusions
\iota_{0}:\bullet\to[0,1]
,
\bullet\mapsto 0
and
\iota_{1}:\bullet\to[0,1]
,
\bullet\mapsto 1
define functors
\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0}),\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1}):\bullet\cong\Pi_{1}(\bullet)\to\Pi_{1}[0,1]
and
for any groupoid
\mathcal{G}
a span of groupoids

\ell_{\mathcal{G}}:=\big(\mathcal{G}\cong\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}\xleftarrow{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})^{*}}\mathcal{G}^{\Pi_{1}[0,1]}\xrightarrow{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})^{*}}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}\cong\mathcal{G}\big).

(10)

As
\langle\iota_{0},\iota_{1}\rangle:\bullet\sqcup\bullet\to\Pi_{1}[0,1]
is a cofibration and
\mathrm{Grpd}
a monoidal model category, with all objects fibrant, the span
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}
is fibrant for any groupoid
\mathcal{G}
. As
\Pi_{1}[0,1]
is contractible,
\mathcal{G}^{\Pi_{1}[0,1]}
is essentially finite for any essentially finite groupoid
\mathcal{G}
.

There is a (unique) natural transformation
[\delta]:\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})\Rightarrow\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})
, defined by the homotopy class of any path
\delta:0\to 1
in
[0,1]
.
Hence for any groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, we have
a natural transformation

[\delta]^{*}:\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})^{*}\Rightarrow\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})^{*}
with components
[\delta]^{*}_{f}=f([\delta])
for any functor
f:\Pi_{1}[0,1]\to\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 2.10
.

For all essentially finite groupoids
\mathcal{G}
and representations
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})^{*}}\mathcal{G}^{\Pi_{1}[0,1]}\xrightarrow{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})^{*}}\mathcal{G})\sim_{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{I}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G}),

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})^{*}}\mathcal{G}^{\Pi_{1}[0,1]}\xrightarrow{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})^{*}}\mathcal{G},\rho[\delta]^{*})\sim_{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}\mathcal{G}^{I}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G},\sigma^{\rho}).

Proof.

We show that these spans are equivalent over
\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}
.
For this, note that
I=\Pi_{1}([0,1],\{0,1\})
and that the inclusion
F:I\to\Pi_{1}[0,1]
defines a morphism of cospans of groupoids

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}(\iota^{\prime}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}(\iota^{\prime}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})}
\textstyle{\Pi_{1}[0,1],}

where
\iota_{i}
and
\iota^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=0,1
denote the maps
\iota_{i}:\{\bullet\}\to[0,1]
,
\bullet\mapsto i
and
\iota^{\prime}_{i}:\{\bullet\}\to\{0,1\}
,
\bullet\mapsto i
.
We choose for each
x\in[0,1]
a path
\gamma_{x}:x\to q_{x}
, where
q_{x}\in\{0,1\}
such that
q_{0}=0
and
q_{1}=1
. By assigning to
x\in[0,1]
the point
q_{x}\in\{0,1\}

and to the homotopy class
[\delta]:x\to y
of a path
\delta
in
[0,1]
the homotopy class
[\gamma_{y}]\circ[\delta]\circ[\gamma_{x}]^{-1}:q_{x}\to q_{y}
, we obtain a functor
F^{\prime}:\Pi_{1}[0,1]\to I
such that
F^{\prime}F=\mathrm{id}_{I}
.
We also obtain a natural isomorphism
g:\mathrm{id}_{\Pi_{1}[0,1]}\Rightarrow FF^{\prime}
with components
g_{x}=[\gamma_{x}]:x\to q_{x}
, such that
g_{0}=1_{0}
and
g_{1}=1_{1}
.
This yields the following equivalence of groupoid spans over
\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}(\iota^{\prime}_{0})^{*}=p_{0}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447ptp_{1}=\Pi_{1}(\iota^{\prime}_{1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{F^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\cong\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}\cong\mathcal{G}.}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}^{\Pi_{1}[0,1]}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})^{*}}

The unique isomorphism
d:0\to 1
in
I=\Pi_{1}([0,1],\{0,1\})
is the homotopy class
d=[\delta]
of any path
\delta:0\to 1
in
[0,1]
.
The natural transformation
\sigma^{\rho}:\rho p_{0}\Rightarrow\rho p_{1}
for a representation
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
is given by
\sigma^{\rho}=\rho[\delta]^{*}:\rho\Pi_{1}(\iota^{\prime}_{0})^{*}\Rightarrow\rho\Pi_{1}(\iota^{\prime}_{1})^{*}
. With the definition of
F
and
F^{\prime}
this yields

\sigma^{\rho}F^{*}=\rho[\delta]^{*}:\rho\Pi_{1}(\iota_{0})^{*}\Rightarrow\rho\Pi_{1}(\iota_{1})^{*}\qquad\qquad\rho[\delta]^{*}F^{\prime*}=\sigma^{\rho}:\rho p_{0}\Rightarrow\rho p_{1}.

∎

2.3
The quantum Quinn construction

In this section we construct a symmetric monoidal functor
L:\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
. This functor will play an important role in the construction of defect TQFTs. As we will show in the following, it
generalises a particular case of the ‘degroupoidification’ functor in
[
BHW
]
. It
can also be seen as a natural generalisation of the groupoid-case of Quinn’s finite total homotopy TQFT
[
Q
, Lecture 4]
, see also
[
G-CKT
, Chapter 8]
, where representations of groupoids are incorporated. This motivates the title of this subsection.

It is also related to the parallel section functor
\mathrm{Par}:\mathrm{VecBun}_{K}\mathrm{Grpd}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{K}
of Schweigert and Woike
[
SW1
, Theorem 3.17]
, where the category
\mathrm{VecBun}_{K}\mathrm{Grpd}
from
[
SW1
, Def 3.7]
is the counterpart of our category
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
. The work
[
SW1
]
in turn uses a more general functor constructed by Trova in
[
Tr
, Theorem 5.1]
and an older construction of such a parallel section functor by Willerton
[
W
]
. The articles
[
SW1
,
Tr
]
build on the work

[
FHLT
]
by Freed, Hopkins Lurie and Teleman, the work
[
Mo
]
by Morton and the work
[
Ha
]
by Haugseng on iterated spans in
(\infty,n)
-categories.

Besides the fact that these references consider
twisted
Dijkgraaf-Witten theory while we restrict attention to the
untwisted
case, they consider categories built from general spans of groupoids, where the composition of spans is defined using homotopy pullbacks
[
BHW
]
. When using general spans, the latter is required as otherwise the pullback groupoid obtained when composing spans would not necessarily be invariant under the equivalence of spans in Lemma
2.7
. As we take pullbacks along fibrations, we already obtain that the pullbacks of spans are invariant under such fibred equivalence of spans, up to fibred homotopy. Therefore, our definition of composition of spans retain the required homotopy-theoretical information. For a general discussion on why pullbacks along fibrations have the correct homotopy type see
[
Hir
, §13.3.1.]
.

One advantage of our construction of
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
is that it
gives a concrete and explicit description.
Moreover, all spans arising in our description of Dijkgraaf-Witten theory are fibrant, and fibrancy has a physical interpretation, namely that gauge transformations extend from the boundary to the bulk.

Our construction requires some known results on groupoid fibrations by Brown
[
Br
, §7.2.1 and §7.2.9]
that we summarise in the following proposition.
Recall from Section
2.1
the notion of a fibration of groupoids. The
fibre
of a functor
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
between groupoids at
B\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{B}
is the groupoid
\mathrm{Fib}(F,B)
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ob}\,\mathrm{Fib}(F,B)=\{A\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{A}\mid F(A)=B\}\qquad\hom_{\mathrm{Fib}(F,B)}(A,A^{\prime})=\{f\in\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A^{\prime})\mid F(f)=1_{B}\}.

For a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and
G\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}
we set
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(G)=\hom_{\mathcal{G}}(G,G)
. We
write
[G]_{\mathcal{G}}=\{G^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}\mid G^{\prime}\cong G\}\subset\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}
for the isomorphism class of
G
, also referred to as its path-component, and

\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G})
for its set of path-components.

Proposition 2.11
.

[
Br
, §7.2.1 and §7.2.9]
Let
p:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a fibration of groupoids.

1.

For any
A\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{A}
with
B=p(A)
there is an exact sequence of groups

\{1\}\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}(A)\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{A}}(A)\xrightarrow{p_{*}}\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B),

where
\iota
is induced by the inclusion
\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
, and
p_{*}
by the fibration
p:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
.

2.

For any
B\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{B}
there is a left action
\rhd:\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B)\times\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Fib}(p,B))\to\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Fib}(p,B))

given by

g\rhd[A]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}=[A_{g}]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}

for any lift
\hat{g}:A_{g}\to A
of
g
.
Furthermore, one has for all
A\in\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)
:

(a)

The action of
p_{*}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{A}}(A))
on
\left[A\right]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}
is trivial.

(b)

The stabiliser of
\left[A\right]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}
is
p_{*}(\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{A}}(A))
,

(c)

The orbit of
\left[A\right]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}

is

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B)\rhd[A]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}=\big\{[A^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}\mid A^{\prime}\in[A]_{\mathcal{A}},p(A)=B\big\},

and for any set of representatives
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{B})
of the path-components of
\mathcal{B}
, there is a bijection

\amalg_{B\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{B})}\;\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Fib}(p,B))/\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B)\to\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}),\quad{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B)\rhd[A]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}\mapsto[A]_{\mathcal{A}}}.

3.

If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are essentially finite, then so is
\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)
for each
B\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{B}
.

4.

The cardinalities of the automorphism groups are related by

|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{A}}(A)|=\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}(A)|\cdot|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B)|}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B)\rhd[A]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}|}.

5.

If
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)
are connected by a morphism in
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}(A)\cong\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}(A^{\prime})
.

Proof.

1. Claim
1
. and claims 2.(a) and 2.(b) are in
[
Br
, §7.2.1 and 7.2.9]
and can be proven by elementary methods. For claim 2.(c), note that if
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)
are in the same path-component in
\mathcal{A}
, then there is a morphism
f:A^{\prime}\to A
in
\mathcal{A}
, and clearly,
p(f)\rhd[A]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}=[A^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}
.

For claim
3
. we only need to prove that
\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)
has a finite number of path-components for all
B\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{B}
. Let
E_{B}
be the full subgroupoid of
\mathcal{A}
with elements
e\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{A}
such that
p(e)\in[B]_{\mathcal{B}}
as objects. If
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ob}E_{B}
are in the same path-component of
\mathcal{A}
, then they are in the same path-component of
E_{B}
. In particular,
E_{B}
has only a finite number of path-components. Choose representatives
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
of the path-components of
E_{B}
. As
p:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a fibration, we can assume that
p(A_{1})=\dots=p(A_{n})=B
. It follows from 2.(c) that the union of the orbits of
[A_{1}]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)},\dots,[A_{n}]_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}

is
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Fib}(p,B))
. As
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{B}}(B)
is finite, this implies that
\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)
is finite.

Claim
4
. follows from the exact sequence in claim
1
. together with the orbit-stabiliser theorem, by applying claim 2.(b). Claim
5
. holds, because any morphism
f:A\to A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
induces an isomorphism

\phi_{f}:\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}(A)\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{Fib}(p,B)}(A^{\prime}),\;h\mapsto fhf^{-1}.

∎

We will now assign to every object
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
of the symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})

a vector space, namely the limit
\lim\rho
, and to each morphism a linear map. To construct the latter, recall
that the limit of a functor
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is given by

\displaystyle\lim\rho=\bigoplus_{G\in R(\mathcal{G})}\;\rho(G)^{\mathrm{Aut}(G)}\qquad\rho(G)^{\mathrm{Aut(G)}}=\{x\in\rho(G)\mid\rho(g)x=x\,\forall g\in\mathrm{Aut}(G)\},

(11)

where
R(\mathcal{G})
is a set of representatives of the isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{G}
.
The inclusions and projections for the direct sums in (
11
)
are denoted

\displaystyle\iota_{G}:\rho(G)^{\mathrm{Aut}(G)}\to\lim\rho\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\pi_{G}:\lim\rho\to\rho(G)^{\mathrm{Aut}(G)}.

(12)

In this formulation the component morphisms
c_{G^{\prime}}:\lim\rho\to\rho(G^{\prime})
of the limit cone are given by
c_{G^{\prime}}\circ\iota_{G}=\rho(f)|_{\rho(G)^{\mathrm{Aut}(G)}}
for any morphism
f:G\to G^{\prime}
with
G\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G})
and
G^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}
.

Consider now a pair
\big(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma\big)\colon(\mathcal{G}_{1},\rho_{1})\to(\mathcal{G}_{2},\rho_{2})
of a fibrant span
\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}
and a natural transformation
\sigma:\rho_{1}P_{1}\Rightarrow\rho_{2}P_{2}

as in Definition
2.8
. Then for any pair of objects
G_{1}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
G_{2}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{2}
and any object
H\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathrm{Fib}\big(\langle P_{1},P_{2}\rangle,(G_{1},G_{2})\big)

we have a linear map

\sigma_{H}:\rho_{1}(G_{1})\to\rho_{2}(G_{2})
.

Lemma 2.12
.

For any
H\in\mathcal{G}
with
P_{1}(H)=G_{1}
and
P_{2}(H)=G_{2}
the linear map
\sigma_{H}:\rho_{1}(G_{1})\to\rho_{2}(G_{2})
depends only on the path-component of
H
in the groupoid
\mathrm{Fib}\big(\langle P_{1},P_{2}\rangle,(G_{1},G_{2})\big)
.

Proof.

By naturality of
\sigma_{H}
the following diagram commutes for any morphism
f:H\to H^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Fib}\big(\langle P_{1},P_{2}\rangle,(G_{1},G_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 257.34772pt\raise-53.435pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}$}}}\kern
