0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-64.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 41.89134pt\raise-64.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 86.78268pt\raise-64.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\rho_{1}P_{1})(H^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 160.03345pt\raise-69.4214pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.6014pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma_{H^{\prime}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 196.22025pt\raise-64.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 166.22025pt\raise-64.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 196.22025pt\raise-64.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\rho_{2}P_{2})(H^{\prime}).}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

As
f:H\to H^{\prime}
is a morphism in the fibre, the vertical arrows are identities, which yields

\sigma_{H}=\sigma_{H^{\prime}}
.
∎

Let

\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2}
be a fibrant span of essentially finite groupoids. As
\langle P_{1},P_{2}\rangle\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
is a fibration and all groupoids are essentially finite, by Proposition
2.11
,
3
. the fibre

\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}:=\mathrm{Fib}\big(\langle P_{1},P_{2}\rangle,(G_{1},G_{2})\big)

is essentially finite for all
G_{1}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
G_{2}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{2}
. Hence, each set
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})
of representatives of its path-components is finite, and all automorphism groups
\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H)
are finite.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let

G_{1}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
G_{2}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{2}
. The following linear map is independent of
the choice of the set
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})
of representatives of path-components of
\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}
and takes values in
\rho_{2}(G_{2})^{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})}

S_{\sigma}^{G_{1},G_{2}}:=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})|}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H)|}\;\sigma_{H}:\rho_{1}(G_{1})\to\rho_{2}(G_{2}).

(19)

Proof.

The first statement follows from Lemma
2.12
.

For the second choose
g\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})
and,
for each
H\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})
, a morphism
g_{H}:H\to H^{\prime}_{g}
in
\mathcal{H}
such that
P_{1}(g_{H})=1_{G_{1}}
and
P_{2}(g_{H})=g
. This is possible
because
\langle P_{1},P_{2}\rangle:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
is a fibration.

By applying Proposition
2.11
,
2
. to the

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{1},G_{2})
-action on
\pi_{0}(\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\})
one finds
that the objects
H^{\prime}_{g}
for
H\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})
form another set of representatives for the path-components of the fibre
\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}
.
We then consider the commuting diagram (
2.3
) for
H^{\prime}=H^{\prime}_{g}
and
f=g_{H}:H\to H^{\prime}_{g}
. In this case, the vertical arrow on the left is given by the identity
\rho_{1}P_{1}(g_{H})=1_{G_{1}}
and the vertical arrow on the right by
\rho_{2}P_{2}(g_{H})=\rho_{2}(g)
.
So the linear map
\rho_{2}(g)\circ S_{\sigma}^{G_{1},G_{2}}:\rho_{1}(G_{1})\to\rho_{2}(G_{2})
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\rho_{2}(g)\circ S_{\sigma}^{G_{1},G_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})|}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H)|}\;\rho_{2}(g)\circ\sigma_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})|}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H)|}\;\sigma_{H^{\prime}_{g}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})|}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H^{\prime}_{g})|}\;\sigma_{H^{\prime}_{g}}=S_{\sigma}^{G_{1},G_{2}},

where we used Proposition
2.11
,
5
. in the last step.
∎

Proposition 2.14
.

There is a symmetric monoidal functor
L:\mathrm{span}^{\rm fib}(\mathrm{repGrpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
that assigns:

•

to an object
(\mathcal{G},\rho)
the vector space
\lim\rho
;

•

to a morphism
[(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{H}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma)]:(\mathcal{G}_{1},\rho_{1})\to(\mathcal{G}_{2},\rho_{2})
the linear map

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\\
G_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})\end{subarray}}\iota_{G_{2}}\circ S_{\sigma}^{G_{1},G_{2}}\circ\pi_{G_{1}}:\lim\rho_{1}\to\lim\rho_{2}.

(20)

Proof.

1. We show that
L
respects the composition of morphisms. For this, we consider a composite

[(\mathcal{G}_{2}\xleftarrow{Q_{1}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{Q_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{3},\tau)]\circ[(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{H}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma)]=[(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}T_{1}}\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{Q_{2}T_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{3},\tau T_{2}\circ\sigma T_{1})].

given by the following commuting diagram, where the middle diamond is a pullback

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{P}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P_{1}}
\scriptstyle{P_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{Q_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{G}_{2}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{3}.}

We then have a fibration
\langle P_{1}T_{1},P,Q_{2}T_{2}\rangle:\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}\times\mathcal{G}_{3}
, by definition of the pullback.
Again by definition of the pullback,
its fibre at
(G_{1},G_{2},G_{2})
is the product
\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}\mid G_{2}\}\times\{G_{2}\mid\mathcal{K}\mid G_{3}\}

of the
fibre of
\langle P_{1},P_{2}\rangle
at
(G_{1},G_{2})
and the fibre of
\langle Q_{1},Q_{2}\rangle
at
(G_{2},G_{3})
.
Moreover,
the restriction
P_{G_{1},G_{3}}:\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}\to\mathcal{G}_{2}
of
P
to the fibre
\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}
of
\langle P_{1}T_{1},Q_{2}T_{2}\rangle:\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}\times{\mathcal{G}_{3}}
is a fibration.

For
G_{i}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{i}
choose sets of representatives
\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H},G_{1},G_{2})
and
\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{K},G_{2},G_{3})
of the path-components of
\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}\mid G_{2}\}
and
\{G_{2}\mid\mathcal{K}\mid G_{3}\}
. Choose also
a set of representatives

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
of the path-components of
\mathcal{G}_{2}
.
As
\langle P_{1}T_{1},P,Q_{2}T_{2}\rangle\colon\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}\times\mathcal{G}_{3}
is a fibration, the set

Y_{G_{1},G_{3}}:=\bigcup_{G_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})}\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}\times\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}

intersects each path-component of
\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}
. It is also clear that for distinct elements
G_{2},G^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
the elements
(H,K)
and
(H^{\prime},K^{\prime})
are in different path-components of
\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}
for all
H\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}
,
H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G^{\prime}_{2}}
and
K\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}
,
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{G^{\prime}_{2},G_{3}}
.
We can therefore choose for each
G_{1}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
G_{3}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}_{3}
a set of representatives
\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{3}}
of the path-components of
\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}
that is contained in
Y_{G_{1},G_{3}}
.

Choose now sets of representatives
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{1})
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{3})
of the path-components in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{3}
. Then we compute for each

G_{1}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{1})
and
G_{3}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{3})

\displaystyle\pi_{G_{3}}\circ

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\tau)\circ\mathcal{L}(\sigma)\circ\iota_{G_{1}}=\sum_{G_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})}S_{\tau}^{G_{2},G_{3}}\circ S_{\sigma}^{G_{1},G_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{G_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})}\quad\sum_{H\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}}\quad\sum_{K\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}}\quad\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(G_{1})|^{-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H)|}\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})|^{-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{2}|\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}}(K)|}\;\tau_{K}\circ\sigma_{H}

\displaystyle=\sum_{G_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})}\quad\sum_{H\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}}\quad\sum_{K\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}}\quad\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(G_{1})|^{-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H)|}\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})|^{-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{2}|\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}}(K)|}\;\big((\tau T_{2})\circ(\sigma T_{1})\big)_{(H,K)}.

Applying Proposition
2.11
,
4
. to the fibration
P_{G_{1},G_{3}}:\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}\to\mathcal{G}_{2}
yields

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}|G_{2}\}}(H)\big|\,\,\big|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{2}|\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}}(K)\big|=\frac{\big|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}}(H,K)\big|\,\,\big|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})\rhd[(H,K)]\big|}{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})}

(21)

for all
H\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}
and
K\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}
,
where

[(H,K)]
is taken in the
G_{2}
-fibre of
P_{G_{1},G_{3}}
.
Hence, we have

\pi_{G_{3}}\circ\mathcal{L}(\tau)\circ\mathcal{L}(\sigma)\circ\iota_{G_{1}}\\
=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(G_{1})|}\sum_{G_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})}\,\sum_{H\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}}\,\sum_{K\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}}\frac{1}{\big|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})\rhd[(H,K)]\big|}\frac{\big((\tau T_{2})\circ(\sigma T_{1})\big)_{(H,K)}}{\big|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}}(H,K)\big|}.

(22)

We now use the following elementary observation: For any finite group
A
acting on a finite set
M
and any
A
-invariant map
f:M\to V
into some vector space
V
, one has

\sum_{[m]\in M/A}f(m)=\sum_{m\in M}\frac{f(m)}{|A\rhd m|}.

We apply this to
the
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})
-action on the path-components of the
G_{2}
-fibre of
P_{G_{1},G_{3}}
in (
22
), which is induced by the
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})
-actions on
\pi_{0}(\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}\mid G_{2}\})
and
\pi_{0}(\{G_{2}\mid\mathcal{K}\mid G_{3}\})
in the product

\pi_{0}\big(\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}\mid G_{2}\}\times\{G_{2}\mid\mathcal{K}\mid G_{3}\}\big)\cong\pi_{0}\big(\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}\mid G_{2}\}\big)\times\pi_{0}\big(\{G_{2}\mid\mathcal{K}\mid G_{3}\}\big)\cong\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{2}}\times\mathcal{R}_{G_{2},G_{3}}.

Proposition
2.11
, 2.(c) gives a bijection

\coprod_{G_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}_{2})}\pi_{0}\big(\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}\mid G_{2}\}\times\{G_{2}\mid\mathcal{K}\mid G_{3}\}\big)/\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})\to\pi_{0}\big(\{G_{1}|\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}|G_{3}\}\big),

and applying this to Equation (
22
) shows that
\mathcal{L}
is compatible with compositions:

\displaystyle\pi_{G_{3}}\circ\mathcal{L}(\tau)\circ\mathcal{L}(\sigma)\circ\iota_{G_{1}}=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(G_{1})|}\sum_{X\in\mathcal{R}_{G_{1},G_{3}}}\frac{1}{\big|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\mathcal{K}\mid G_{3}\}}(X)\big|}\;\big((\tau T_{2})\circ(\sigma T_{1})\big)_{X}.

3. To show that
L
respects identity morphisms, we consider the identity span
[(\mathcal{G}\xleftarrow{p_{0}}{\mathcal{G}}^{I}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G},\sigma^{\rho})]
on
(\mathcal{G},\rho)
with
\sigma^{\rho}_{f}=\rho(f)
for all morphisms
f
in
\mathcal{G}
. Choose a set
\mathcal{R}(G)

of representatives of the path-components of
\mathcal{G}
. Then we have
\hom_{\mathcal{G}}(G_{1},G_{2})=\emptyset
, for
G_{1},G_{2}\in R(\mathcal{G})
, with
G_{1}\neq G_{2}
. It follows that

\pi_{G_{2}}\circ\mathcal{L}(\sigma^{\rho})\circ\iota_{G_{1}}=0.

On the other hand,
for any
G\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{G}
the fibre
\{G|\mathcal{G}^{I}|G\}=\mathrm{Fib}(\langle p_{0},p_{1}\rangle,(G,G))
has only identity morphisms, and exactly
|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(G)|
components. We can thus choose

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G}^{I},G,G)=\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(G)
and obtain

\displaystyle\pi_{G}\circ\mathcal{L}(\sigma^{\rho})\circ\iota_{G}=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(G)|}\sum_{f\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(G)}\sigma^{\rho}_{f}=\mathrm{id}_{\rho(G)^{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}}(G)}}.

4. Let us now address independence on representatives. Suppose that

(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{P_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma)\sim_{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{P^{\prime}_{1}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2},\sigma^{\prime}).

Then there are functors
F:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
with
P^{\prime}_{i}F=P_{i}
,
P_{i}F^{\prime}=P^{\prime}_{i}
that form an equivalence over
\mathcal{G}_{1}\times\mathcal{G}_{2}
and satisfy
\sigma^{\prime}F=\sigma
and
\sigma F^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}
. Then for any
x_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{i}
the restriction
F_{x_{i}}:\mathrm{Fib}(P_{i},x_{i})\to\mathrm{Fib}(P^{\prime}_{i},x_{i})
is an equivalence of categories.
Applying this to (
19
) and using
\sigma^{\prime}F=\sigma
and
\sigma F^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}
shows that
\mathcal{L}(\sigma)=\mathcal{L}(\sigma^{\prime})
.

5. Compatibility with the symmetric monoidal structure is clear.
∎

The symmetric monoidal functor
L:\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from Proposition
2.14
generalises the symmetric monoidal functor
L_{\mathrm{class}}:\mathrm{span}(\mathrm{Grpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
for Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT without defects, constructed in a more general setting by Quinn in
[
Q
, Lecture 4]
, see
also Freed and Quinn
[
FQ
]
and Section 4 in Faria Martins and Porter
[
FMP2
]
.
If we identify the symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})
from Lemma
2.7
with a subcategory of
\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{repGrpd})
as in Remark
2.9
, then the restriction of
L
to this subcategory defines a functor
L_{\mathrm{class}}:\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Corollary 2.15
.

There is a symmetric monoidal functor
L_{\mathrm{class}}:\mathrm{span}^{\mathrm{fib}}(\mathrm{Grpd})\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
that assigns

•

to an essentially finite groupoid
\mathcal{G}
the free complex vector space
L_{\mathcal{G}}=\langle\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
generated by
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G})
,

•

to a fibrant span
s=(\mathcal{G}_{1}\xleftarrow{p_{1}}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{2})
the linear map
L_{s}:\langle\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\to\langle\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
with matrix elements

\langle[G_{1}]\mid L_{s}\mid[G_{2}]\rangle=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(G_{2})|}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{G},G_{1},G_{2})}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{G}\mid G_{2}\}}(G)|}.

The formula for the matrix elements in Corollary
2.15
can be cast in a simpler form by using the homotopy content or groupoid cardinality of an essentially finite groupoid
\mathcal{H}
, see Baez, Hoffnung and Walker
[
BHW
]

\chi^{\pi}(\mathcal{H})=\sum_{H\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{H})}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{H}}(H)|}.

Denoting by
\mathrm{PC}_{H}(\mathcal{H})
for
H\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{H}
the full subgroupoid of
\mathcal{H}
with object set
[H]_{\mathcal{H}}
, we then have

\langle[G_{1}]\mid L_{s}\mid[G_{2}]\rangle=\chi^{\pi}\big(\mathrm{PC}_{G_{2}}(\mathcal{G}_{2})\big)\cdot\chi^{\pi}\big(\{G_{1}\mid\mathcal{G}\mid G_{2}\}\big).

This
arises as a specialisation of the functor constructed by Quinn in
[
Q
, Lecture 4]
to the case where the target spaces are given by classifying spaces of groupoids. For a more detailed discussion see
[
FMP2
, §3.3, §4.3 and §8.2]
and
[
FMP1
]
.

3
Stratifications and graded graphs

3.1
Graded graphs

In the following all graphs are assumed to be finite.
We consider the category
\mathrm{Graph}
, whose objects are finite graphs,
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t:Q_{1}\to Q_{0})
, and whose morphisms
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
are graph maps, given by maps
f_{0}:Q_{0}\to Q^{\prime}_{0}
and
f_{1}:Q_{1}\to Q^{\prime}_{1}
with
s^{\prime}\circ f_{1}={f_{0}\circ s}
and
t^{\prime}\circ f_{1}=f_{0}\circ t
. The forgetful functor
R:\mathrm{Cat}\to\mathrm{Graph}
has a left adjoint

L:\mathrm{Graph}\to\mathrm{Cat}
that assigns to a graph
Q
the
category
\mathcal{Q}=L(Q)
freely generated by
Q
and to a graph map
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
the induced functor
F=L(f):\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

A
path
of length
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
in a graph
Q
is a finite sequence
q=(e_{k},\ldots,e_{1})
of edges
e_{i}\in Q_{1}
with
s(e_{i})=t(e_{i-1})
for
i=2,\ldots,k
. We write
s(q)=s(e_{1})
and
t(q)=t(e_{k})
for the starting and target vertex of
q
and
q:s(q)\to t(q)
. For
v\in Q_{0}
, we denote by
Q^{k}(v)
the set of paths of length
k
starting in
v
and by
Q(v)=\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}Q^{k}(v)
the set of all paths starting in
v
.

The
subgraph generated
by
v\in Q_{0}
is the graph
Q^{v}
with vertex and edge sets

Q^{v}_{0}=\{t(q)\mid q\in Q(v)\}\qquad Q^{v}_{1}=\{e\in Q_{1}\mid s(e)\in Q^{v}_{0}\}.

The maps
s,t:Q^{v}_{1}\to Q^{v}_{0}
are induced
by the ones of
Q
and the inclusion
i_{v}:Q^{v}\to Q
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
graded graph

(Q,\deg)
is a graph
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t:Q_{1}\to Q_{0})
together with a
degree map

\deg:Q_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
\deg\circ t=\deg\circ s-1
.

Definition 3.2
.

An
insertion

f:(Q,\deg)\to(Q^{\prime},\deg^{\prime})
is a graph map
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
that

(i)

preserves degree:
\deg^{\prime}\circ f_{0}=\deg
,

(ii)

induces graph isomorphisms
f_{v}:Q^{v}\to Q^{\prime f_{0}(v)}
for all
v\in Q_{0}
.

Clearly, the identity map
\mathrm{id}_{Q}:(Q,\deg)\to(Q,\deg)
on a graded graph
Q
is an insertion. Condition (ii) implies that for an insertion
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
, any edge
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}_{1}
with
s^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=v^{\prime}\in f_{0}(Q_{0})
satisfies
q^{\prime}\in f_{1}(Q_{1})
. More specifically, for each
v\in f_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})
, there is a unique edge
q_{v}\in Q_{1}
with
s(q_{v})=v
and
f_{1}(q_{v})=q^{\prime}
.
Hence, for any path
q^{\prime}:y^{\prime}\to z^{\prime}
in
Q^{\prime}
with
y^{\prime}\in f_{0}(Q_{0})
and any
y\in f_{0}^{-1}(y^{\prime})
, there is a unique path
q_{y}:y\to z
whose image is
q
. This implies that the functor
F:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
induced by an insertion
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
is a discrete opfibration in the sense of
[
LR
, Section 2.1]
.

Definition 3.3
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
between small categories is called
a
discrete opfibration
if
for every
q^{\prime}\in\hom_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})
and
y\in F^{-1}(y^{\prime})
there is a unique morphism

q_{y}
in
\mathcal{Q}
with source
y
and
F(q_{y})=q^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For any insertion
f:(Q,\deg)\to(Q^{\prime},\deg^{\prime})
the
functor
F:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is
a discrete opfibration.

In the following we will consider graded graphs that have at least one vertex of degree
0
and such that each vertex of degree 1 has exactly two outgoing edges. These edges come in two types.

Definition 3.5
.

A graded graph
(Q,\deg)
is called
regular
, if
each vertex of degree
>0
has at least one outgoing edge and each vertex of degree
1
has exactly two outgoing edges.

A choice of
normals
for a regular graph
is a partition
(\deg\circ s)^{-1}(1)=L\dot{\cup}R
such that
each vertex
v\in Q_{0}
with
\deg(v)=1
has
one outgoing edge,
l(v):v\to L(v)
, in
L
and the other one,
r(v):v\to R(v)
, in
R
.

3.2
Stratifications

3.2.1
Homogeneous stratifications

Throughout the article we work in the piecewise linear (PL) framework, see for instance Rourke and Sanderson
[
RS
]
and Friedman
[
Fr
, Appendix B]
. All manifolds and mappings are PL. Each compact PL manifold is a compact polyhedron and the geometric realisation of a finite simplicial complex. For every
PL map between PL manifolds, there is a locally-finite decomposition of the domain into simplexes such that
the map is affine linear on each simplex. Every PL map arises from a simplicial map between simplicial complexes.

All manifolds in this article are oriented PL manifolds of dimension
n\leq 3
, possibly open and with boundaries. Their boundaries are oriented such that their normals
point outwards. For better legibility we write
n
-manifold
for an oriented PL manifold of dimension
n\leq 3
.
A (not necessarily connected) closed 2-manifold is called a
surface
.

We define a
filtration
of a Hausdorff space
X
as in
[
Fr
, Def 2.2.1]
as a sequence of closed subspaces
\emptyset=X^{-1}\subset X^{0}\subset\ldots\subset X^{n}=X
for some integer
n\geq-1
. The space
X^{k}
is called
k
-skeleton
, the connected components of
X^{k}\setminus X^{k-1}
are called the
k
-strata
and
n
is called the
formal dimension
of
X
. A homeomorphism
f:X\to Y
between filtered spaces
X,Y
of the same formal dimension is called a
filtered homeomorphism
if
f(X^{k})\subset Y^{k}
for all
k=0,\ldots,n
, see
[
Fr
, Def 2.3.2]
. Note that any open subset
U\subset X
of a filtered space
X
inherits a filtration with
k
-skeleta
U^{k}=U\cap X^{k}
.

Definition 3.6
.

A
stratification
of an
n
-dimensional PL manifold
X
is a filtration by closed PL subspaces

\emptyset=X^{-1}\subset X^{0}\subset X^{1}\subset\ldots\subset X^{n}=X

such that
X^{k}\setminus X^{k-1}
is a
k
-dimensional PL manifold with boundary
\partial(X^{k}\setminus X^{k-1})\subset\partial X
.
The
codimension
of a
k
-stratum
s
is
\mathrm{codim}(s)=n-k
, and the set of
k
-strata is denoted
S^{X}_{k}
.

We call
0-strata
vertices
, 1-strata
edges
, 2-strata
planes
and 3-strata
regions
of
X
.
For
n=3
, a 1-stratum that is PL homeomorphic to
S^{1}
is called an
isolated loop
.

Note that the empty manifold is a
k
-manifold for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Thus
X_{k}=X_{k-1}
is allowed, and in this case there are no
k
-strata.
As explained for instance in
[
AH
]
, see also
[
Fr
, Lemma 2.5.12]
, for each
stratification of a compact
n
-manifold
X
there is a simplicial complex
C
with underlying polyhedron
X
such that all skeleta of
X
are subcomplexes of
C
. This follows, because
X
and all
k
-skeleta
X^{k}
are
compact polyhedra and hence underlying polyhedra of simplicial complexes
D
and
D^{k}

[
RS
, Theorem 2.11]
. By
[
RS
, Addendum 2.12]
there are simplicial subdivisions
C
of
D
and
C^{k}
of
D^{k}
such that all
C^{k}
are subcomplexes of
C
. This implies that for each
k
the number of
k
-strata is finite, in
particular
X^{0}
is a finite discrete set.

We also need a condition that ensures that neighbourhoods of points on a stratum are isomorphic and that the stratification is transversal at the boundary. This is the homogeneity condition below, which
is equivalent to the condition called homogeneous in
[
BMS
, Def 2.24]
, which in turn restricts the condition called CS in
[
S
,
AH
]
.
For PL manifolds without boundary, imposing this condition yields a special case of the PL stratified pseudomanifolds in
[
Fr
, Def. 2.5.13]
.

In addition to homogeneity we also impose a further condition which is not present in
[
BMS
, Def 2.24]
. It is imposed to ensure
that every
k
-stratum is contained in the closure of at least one
l
-stratum for all
k\leq l\leq n
.

For an
m
-sphere
S
we denote by
cS
the (open) cone over
S
. Any stratification of
S
induces a stratification of
cS
with skeleta
(cS)^{k+1}=c(S^{k})
and the apex
v_{c}
of the cone as
0
-skeleton. The products
cS\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
cS\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
of this cone with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and with the closed half-space
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid x_{n}\geq 0\}
are also stratified manifolds, in which each
(n+k)
-stratum is a product of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
with a
k
-stratum of
cS
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
X
be a stratified manifold. A
k
-stratum
s
is called
homogeneous
if there is a stratification of the
(n-k-1)
-sphere
S
such that:

1.

For any point
x\in s\cap\partial X
there is an open neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
in
X
and a filtered PL homeomorphism

h:(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}\to U_{x}
with
h(v_{c},0)=x
, whose inverse is a filtered PL homeomorphism.

2.

For any point
x\in s\setminus\partial X
there is an open neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
in
X
and a filtered PL homeomorphism
h:(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to U_{x}
with
h(v_{c},0)=x
, whose inverse is a filtered PL homeomorphism.

The filtered PL homeomorphisms
h
are called
local charts
and the open neighbourhoods
U_{x}

special neighbourhoods
of
x
. The stratified sphere
S
is called the
link
of
s
in
X
.

A homogeneous
k
-stratum is called
saturated
, if the stratified
(n-k-1)
-sphere
S

has at least one
j
-stratum for
j=0,...,n-k-1
.
A stratification is homogeneous or saturated, if all strata are homogeneous or saturated.

As we work in the PL setting, the link of each stratum
s
is determined uniquely, up to isomorphisms of stratified spheres, see for instance
[
Fr
, Lemma 2.5.18]
.

As discussed in
[
BMS
]
, the homogeneity condition is automatically satisfied for all
n
-strata and
0
-strata. In particular, one has
S^{0}=\{-1,1\}
and
S^{-1}=\emptyset
, whose cone is
\{\bullet\}
, so the homogeneity condition is void for
k=n
.
It is equivalent to local flatness for
(n-1)
-strata, which holds for
n\leq 4
by the Schönflies theorem. Thus, every stratification of a manifold of dimension
n\leq 2
is automatically homogeneous. A stratification of a manifold of dimension
n=3
can be made homogeneous by adding vertices to its 1-skeleton
[
AH
,
BMS
]
.

Likewise, all
n
-strata and
(n-1)
-strata in a stratification of an
n
-manifold are automatically saturated.
Stratifications that are triangulations or dual to triangulations are always homogeneous and saturated.

As we consider such manifolds only for dimensions
2
and
3
, we can list all types of neighbourhoods of strata and show examples in Figure
1
.
For
n=2
the stratified spaces
N=(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}
are

•

k=2
: the half-space
N=\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
with
N^{0}=N^{1}=\emptyset
,

•

k=1
: the strip
N=[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
N^{0}=\emptyset
and
N^{1}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

•

k=0
: a disc
N=D^{2}
with
N^{0}=\{0\}
and radial 1-strata intersecting in
N^{0}
.

For
n=3
, the stratified spaces
(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}
are

•

k=3
: the half-space
N=\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
with
N^{0}=N^{1}=N^{2}=\emptyset
,

•

k=2
: the space
N=[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
with
N^{0}=N^{1}=\emptyset
and
N^{2}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
,

•

k=1
:
N=D^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
N^{0}=\emptyset
,
N^{1}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
and 2-strata intersecting in
N^{1}
,

•

k=0
: a ball
N=D^{3}
with
N^{0}=\{0\}
and 2-strata intersecting in radial 1-strata.

Note that the saturation condition requires that for
n=2
and
k=0
the special neighbourhoods from Definition
3.7
contain at least one
1
-stratum and for
n=3
and
k=0,1
they contain at least one 1-stratum and at least one 2-stratum.
Note also that the saturation condition applies to all points in the special neighbourhoods from Definition
3.7
, including the ones on higher-dimensional strata.
It follows that each
l
-stratum in the special neighbourhoods from Definition
3.7
is contained in the closure of at least one
(l+1)
-stratum and that each
(l+1)
-stratum with
l\geq k
contains at least one
l
-stratum in its closure. Note also that the unique
k
-stratum in these neighbourhoods is contained in the closure of all
l
-strata with
l\geq k
.

Figure 1:
Neighbourhoods of
k
-strata in a stratified
n
-manifold for
k<n
. The maps
\iota_{k}:S_{k}^{\partial X}\to S_{k+1}^{X}
from (
23
) are induced by the inclusion

\iota:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(x_{1},x_{2},0)
.

3.2.2
Boundary stratifications

Let
X
be an
n
-manifold with a homogeneous and saturated stratification.
Then the homogeneity condition implies that each
k
-stratum of
X
is transversal to
\partial X
, so that no
0
-stratum of
X
is contained in
\partial X
.
It also ensures that a stratification of
X
induces a homogeneous and saturated stratification of
\partial X
.
If
x\in\partial X\cap s
for a
k
-stratum
s
, then any local chart
h:cS\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to U_{x}
restricts to a local chart

h:cS\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\to U_{x}\cap\partial X
.

Lemma 3.8
.

A homogeneous and saturated stratification of an
n
-manifold
X
induces a homogeneous and saturated stratification of
\partial X

\emptyset=\partial X^{-1}\subset\partial X^{1}\subset\ldots\subset\partial X^{n-1}=\partial X\qquad\qquad\partial X^{k}=X^{k+1}\cap\partial X.

The subspaces
\partial X^{k}
are called
boundary
k
-skeleta
, and the connected components of
\partial X^{k}\setminus\partial X^{k-1}
are called
boundary
k
-strata
. We denote by
S^{\partial X}_{k}
the set of boundary
k
-strata.

Note that boundary
k
-strata of a stratified
n
-manifold
X

are not

k
-strata of
X
. In fact, they are associated with
(k+1)
-strata of
X
.
The homogeneity condition implies that for each boundary
k
-stratum
s\in S_{k}^{\partial X}
there is a unique
(k+1)
-stratum
t\in S_{k+1}^{X}
with
s\subset t
. This defines (not necessarily injective) maps

\displaystyle\iota_{k}:S_{k}^{\partial X}\to S_{k+1}^{X}

(23)

that respect the closures of strata: if a boundary
k
-stratum
s
is contained in the closure of a boundary
l
-stratum
t
, then
\iota_{k}(s)
is contained in the closure of
\iota_{l}(t)
.

3.2.3
Oriented, framed and fine stratifications

In the following we require that all strata of codimension
<3
in a stratified
n
-manifold
X
are oriented. The
n
-strata and boundary
(n-1)
-strata inherit an orientation from
X
. For the other strata, it is a datum.

Definition 3.9
.

1.

An
oriented stratification
of a surface
\Sigma
is a homogeneous and saturated stratification of
\Sigma
together with the choice of an orientation
for each edge and vertex.

2.

An
oriented stratification
of a 3-manifold
M

is a homogeneous and saturated stratification of
M
together with the choice of an orientation for each plane and edge.

For
(n-1)
-strata, orientation is encoded in the choice of a normal.

Note that
an oriented stratification of
X
induces an oriented stratification of
\partial X
.
Vertices of
\partial X
that correspond to starting and target ends of edges in
X
have negative and positive orientation, respectively, if the normal at
\partial X
is oriented outwards.

For an
(n-1)
-stratum
s
in an oriented stratification of an
n
-manifold
X
we denote by
R(s)
and
L(s)
the
n
-strata at the starting and target end of the normal of
s
. For an edge
e
in a stratified 3-manifold
X
that is not an isolated loop, we denote by

s(e)
,
t(e)
the starting and target vertex or boundary vertex of
e
.

We also impose that all strata of codimension 2 are equipped with a framing. This is needed to ensure that all the bundles of local strata defined in the following subsection are trivial. As there are stratified manifolds with non-trivial bundles of local strata, cf. Example
3.14
, not all stratified manifolds admit a framing.

A framing of a codim 2-stratum
s
is a coherent choice of an
n
-stratum in each special neighbourhood
U_{x}
of each point
x\in s
from Definition
3.7
. Recall that
U_{x}
inherits a filtration from
X
and that its
n
-strata are the connected components of
U_{x}\cap(X^{n}\setminus X^{n-1})
.
Coherent means that these choices coincide, whenever the special neighbourhoods intersect in a point on
s
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
X
be an
n\leq 3
-manifold with an oriented homogeneous and saturated stratification.

1.

A
framing
of an
(n-2)
-stratum
s\in S^{X}_{n-2}
is a choice of an
n
-stratum
t_{U_{x}}
in each special neighbourhood
U_{x}
of each point
x\in s
, such that

t_{U_{x}}\cap W=t_{U_{y}}\cap W
for all special neighbourhoods
U_{x}
,
U_{y}
of
x,y\in s
and
neighbourhoods
W
of a point
z\in s\cap U_{x}\cap U_{y}
.

2.

The stratification is called
framed
, if each
(n-2)
-stratum is equipped with a framing.

Note that any framed oriented homogeneous stratification of a 3-manifold
X
induces a framed oriented homogeneous stratification of its boundary
\partial X
. The framing of the 0-strata of
\partial X
is induced by the framing of the 1-strata of
X
.

Convention:
In the following we mean by
stratified
n
-manifold
an
n
-manifold with a
framed

oriented

homogeneous
and
saturated
stratification, where
n\in\{2,3\}
.
All stratifications considered hereafter satisfy these conditions unless stated otherwise.

Embeddings and homeomorphisms of stratified
n
-manifolds
are filtered PL embeddings and PL homeomorphisms that preserve the orientations of the manifolds and of all strata and the framings of all codimension 2-strata.

It will sometimes be useful to consider stratifications in which all strata and boundary strata are open balls and every stratum is incident to at least one vertex or boundary vertex. This holds for instance for stratifications that are triangulations or dual to triangulations of
X
.

Definition 3.11
.

A framed homogeneous and saturated stratification of an
n
-manifold
X
is called
fine
, if

(i)

all
k
-strata and boundary
k
-strata of
X
are open
k
-balls,

(ii)

the closure of each stratum contains at least one vertex or boundary vertex,

(iii)

it induces a fine stratification of
\partial X
.

3.2.4
Bundles of local strata

A stratified
n
-manifold
X
comes with a notion of
local
j
-strata
at any stratum and at any point of
X
. The latter are essentially germs, but can be described in a
more direct way in our setting.

Recall that any point
x\in X
is contained in a unique
k
-stratum
s
of
X
. Any special neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x\in s
as in Definition
3.7
inherits a filtration by closed subspaces from
X
. Its
j
-strata are the connected components of
U_{x}\cap(X^{j}\setminus X^{j-1})
for
j\geq k
. They are in bijection with
(j-k)
-strata in the cone
cS
and with
j
-strata in the spaces
cS\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
or
cS\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}
from Definition
3.7
.

Given two special neighbourhoods
U_{x}
and
V_{x}
of
x\in s
, we identify a
j
-stratum
p
of
U_{x}
and a
j
-stratum
q
of
V_{x}
, whenever there is a neighbourhood
W
of
x
such that
p\cap W=q\cap W
. This is an equivalence relation on the set of all
j
-strata of special neighbourhoods of
x
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
X
be a stratified
n
-manifold,
s
a stratum of
X
and
x\in s
.
A
local
j
-stratum
at
x
is an equivalence class of
j
-strata of special neighbourhoods of
x
.

By the homogeneity condition, the number of local
j
-strata at a point on a
k
-stratum
s
depends only on
s
. Hence, there is a bijection between local
j
-strata at
x
and at
y
for all
x,y\in s
. However, for stratifications without framings or without orientations, this bijection need not be canonical.

The local
j
-strata at points
x\in s
define a locally trivial bundle
\pi_{j}:E_{j}\to s
with typical fibre
\{1,\ldots,l_{j}\}
, where
l_{j}
is the number of local
j
-strata at any point
x\in s
.
A local trivialisation is given by the local charts of
s
. For each
x\in s
, there is a neighbourhood
W_{x}
of
x
and a PL map
H:W_{x}\times(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to X
such that for each
y\in W_{x}
the map
z\mapsto H(y,z)
is a local chart, with domain either
(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, if
z\in s\setminus\partial X
, or
(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}
, if
z\in\partial X\cap s
. This follows,
because it holds for the stratified manifolds
(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}
in Figure
1
. The transition functions between the charts are given by the identifications of the local strata with strata in
(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
or
(cS)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}
. The fibre bundle construction theorem, see for instance
[
Hu
, Theorem 3.2]
, yields the fibre bundle.

In our situation the bundle
\pi_{j}:E_{j}\to s
of local
j
-strata at a
k
-stratum
s
is trivial, i.e. of the form
E_{j}=s\times\pi_{j}^{-1}(x)
for any
x\in s
.
This holds trivially for
k=0
, where
s
contains a single point, and for
k=j
, where there is a single local
k
-stratum at each point
x\in s
.
For
k=n-1
and
j=n
, we have a canonical identification of local
n
-strata at any two points
x,y\in s
, because the orientation of
s
gives a globally defined normal on
s
. This shows that
E_{n}=s\times\pi_{n}^{-1}(x)
for all
x\in s
.
It remains to consider
k=1
and
n=3
, where
s
is either an interval or an isolated loop.
If
s
is an interval, then the bundle of local
j
-strata is trivial, as
s
is contractible. If
s
is an isolated loop, the framing defines a trivialisation.

The triviality of
\pi_{j}:E_{j}\to s
gives a canonical identification between local strata at any two points
x,y\in s
.
This allows us to define
j
-local strata
of the stratum

s
as path-components of the total space of the bundle
\pi_{j}:E_{j}\to s
and to canonically identify them with
local strata
at any point

x\in s
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
X
be a stratified
n
-manifold.
A
local
j
-stratum of a stratum
s
is a path-component of the bundle
\pi_{j}:E_{j}\to s
. The set of local
j
-strata at
s
is denoted
S^{loc}_{j}(s)=\pi_{0}(E_{j})
.
The unique element of
q\cap\pi_{j}^{-1}(x)
for a local stratum
q
at
s
and
x\in s
is called the
representative
of
q
at
x
.

Example 3.14
.

The existence of framings is a necessary condition for the triviality of the bundles of local 2- and 3-strata of an isolated loop. Let

X=D^{2}\times S^{1}
be the stratified 3-manifold with boundary
\partial X=S^{1}\times S^{1}
obtained by gluing the top and bottom face of the following stratified solid cylinder with a rotation by
\pi
.

s
r
\pi
t

Then
X^{1}=r=S^{1}
,
X^{2}=s\cup r
is a Möbius strip and
X^{3}=D^{2}\times S^{1}
a solid torus. Note that
s=X^{2}\setminus X^{1}
is orientable, so this stratification of
D^{2}\times S^{1}
can indeed be oriented.
Every
point

x\in r
has one local 1-stratum, two local 2-strata and two local 3-strata, but
the
stratum

r
has only one local 1-, 2- and 3-stratum.
In this case, the isolated loop cannot be framed.

Note that local
j
-strata at a stratum
s

are not necessarily in bijection with
j
-strata
t
such that
s\subset\bar{t}
. For instance, for a vertex
v
the
local 1-strata
at
v
are the
edge ends
incident at
v
, while the
1-strata
at
v
are the
edges
incident at
v
. For each
k
-stratum
s
and each
j\geq k
there is a map

T_{s,j}:S^{loc}_{j}(s)\to S^{X}_{j}

that assigns to a local
j
-stratum at
s
the associated
j
-stratum of
X
. In general, this map is not injective.

Definition 3.15
.

We write
q:s\to t
for a
local
j
-stratum
q\in S^{loc}_{j}(s)
with associated
j
-stratum
t=T_{s,j}(q)
.

.

Consider the following stratification of a 2-sphere with a single 0-stratum
x
, 1-strata
t,u,v,w
and 2-strata
S,T,U,V,W
.

x
w
W
w_{1}
w_{2}
t
T

u
v
V
v_{1}
v_{2}
S
U_{1}
U_{2}

t_{1}

S_{3}

The dashed disc is a neighbourhood of

as in Definition

, and the local strata at

are

•

a single local 0-stratum
x:x\to x
,

•

local 1-strata
t_{1},t_{2}:x\to t
,

,
v_{1},v_{2}:x\to v

3.2.5
Maps between sets of local strata

For any local
k
-stratum
q:s\to t
and
j\geq k
, any local
j
-stratum at
t
defines a local
j
-stratum at
s
. Intuitively, this follows, because
s\subset\bar{t}
and hence any stratum at
t
is also a stratum at
s
, and because all bundles of local strata considered in this article are trivial.

Lemma 3.17
.

Every local stratum
q:s\to t
in a stratified
n
-manifold
X
induces maps

S^{q}_{j}:S^{loc}_{j}(t)\to S^{loc}_{j}(s)\qquad\qquad{j\in\{0,1,2,3\}.}

Proof.

Let
t
be a
k
-stratum of
X
.
For each
x\in s
there is a unique local
k
-stratum at
x
that represents
q
, and for each special neighbourhood
U_{x}
as in Definition
3.7
, there is a unique stratum
q^{\prime}
of
U_{x}
that represents
q
.

For any
y\in q^{\prime}
we can choose a neighbourhood
U_{y}
as in Definition
3.7
with
U_{y}\subset U_{x}
. This identifies every
j
-stratum
w_{y}
in
U_{y}
with a unique
j
-stratum
w_{x}
in
U_{x}
and defines a map
S^{q}_{j}:S^{loc}_{j}(t,y)\to S^{loc}_{j}(s,x)
,
w_{y}\mapsto w_{x}
, where

S^{loc}_{j}(t,y)
and
S^{loc}_{j}(s,x)
denote the sets of local
j
-strata at
x
and
y
, respectively. Clearly, this does not depend on the choice of the special neighbourhoods
U_{x}
and
U_{y}
. As the bundle of local
j
-strata of
t
is trivial, it also does not depend on the choice of
y\in q^{\prime}
and defines a map
S^{q}_{j}:S^{loc}_{j}(t)\to\pi_{j}^{-1}(x)
.
By passing to the path-components of the bundle of local
j
-strata over
s
, we obtain a
map
S^{q}_{j}:S^{loc}_{j}(t)\to S_{j}^{loc}(s)
that does not depend on the choice of
x\in s
, as this bundle is trivial.
∎

Example 3.18
.

For the stratification in Example
3.16

we have

S^{loc}_{0}(x)=\{x\}\qquad S^{loc}_{1}(x)=\{t_{1},t_{2},u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2},w_{1},w_{2}\}\qquad S^{loc}_{2}(x)=\{S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},T,U_{1},U_{2},V,W\}.

The maps associated with the local 1-stratum
u_{1}:x\to u
are given by

S_{0}^{u_{1}}=\emptyset:\emptyset\to S_{0}^{loc}(x),\qquad S_{1}^{u_{1}}:\{u\}\to S^{loc}_{1}(x),\;u\mapsto u_{1}\qquad S^{u_{1}}_{2}:\{U,S\}\to S^{loc}_{2}(x),\;U\mapsto U_{1},S\mapsto S_{3},

and the maps associated with the local 1-stratum
u_{2}:x\to u
by

S_{0}^{u_{2}}=\emptyset:\emptyset\to S_{0}^{loc}(x),\qquad S_{1}^{u_{2}}:\{u\}\to S^{loc}_{1}(x),\;u\mapsto u_{2}\qquad S^{u_{2}}_{2}:\{U,S\}\to S^{loc}_{2}(x),\;U\mapsto U_{2},S\mapsto S_{1}.

In the following we will sometimes describe local strata
q:s\to t
at a
k
-stratum
s
by composable sequences of local strata.
In particular, we consider sequence of local strata that each raise the dimension by one.

Definition 3.19
.

A sequence of local strata

s=u_{0}\xrightarrow{q_{1}}u_{1}\to\ldots\xrightarrow{q_{m}}u_{m}=t
in a stratified
n
-manifold
X

represents
a local stratum
q:s\to t
, if the induced maps between the sets of local
j
-strata agree:

S_{j}^{q}=S_{j}^{q_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ S_{j}^{q_{m}}:S^{loc}_{j}(t)\to S^{loc}_{j}(s)\qquad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Lemma 3.20
.

For each
k
-stratum
s
of
X
and local
l
-stratum
q:s\to t
, there is a sequence

s=u_{k}\xrightarrow{q_{k+1}}u_{k+1}\xrightarrow{q_{k+2}}\ldots\xrightarrow{q_{l-1}}u_{l-1}\xrightarrow{q_{l}}u_{l}=t

of local strata that represents
q
and such that each local stratum raises the dimension by one.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the saturation condition.
It holds trivially for all local
l
-strata
q
with
l=k+1
. If
l>k+1
, choose a neighbourhood
U_{x}
of a point
x\in s
as in Definition
3.7
and an
l
-stratum
q^{\prime}
in
U_{x}
that represents
q
. Then by the discussion at the end of Section
3.2.1
there is an
(l-1)
-stratum
u^{\prime}_{l-1}\subset\overline{q}^{\prime}
in
U_{x}
with
s\subset\overline{u}^{\prime}_{l-1}
. If
l=k+2
, then
s\to u_{l-1}\to t
is the required sequence of local strata, where
u_{l-1}=[u^{\prime}_{l-1}]
denotes the path-component of
u^{\prime}_{l-1}
.
If
l=k+3
, there is an
(l-2)
-stratum
u^{\prime}_{l-2}\subset\overline{u}^{\prime}_{l-1}
in
U_{x}
with
s\subset\overline{u}^{\prime}_{l-2}
,
and

s\to u_{l-2}\to u_{l-1}\to t
with
u_{i}=[u^{\prime}_{i}]
is the required sequence of local strata.
∎

Example 3.21
.

For the stratification in Example
3.16

•

the local 2-stratum
U_{1}:x\to U
is represented by

x\xrightarrow{u_{1}}u\xrightarrow{U}U
and
x\xrightarrow{t_{1}}t\xrightarrow{U}U
,

•

the local 2-stratum
S_{3}:x\to S
is represented by

x\xrightarrow{u_{1}}u\xrightarrow{S}S

,

•

the local 2-stratum
W:x\to W
is represented by

x\xrightarrow{w_{1}}w\xrightarrow{W}W
and
x\xrightarrow{w_{2}}w\xrightarrow{W}W
.

Finally, note that for a stratified 3-manifold
X
and the associated boundary stratification of
\partial X
, the inclusions

\iota_{k}:S_{k}^{\partial X}\to S_{k+1}^{X}
from (
23
) respect local strata. For any boundary
k
-stratum
s
, there are canonical bijections

\displaystyle\iota_{kj}:S^{loc}_{j}(s)\to S^{loc}_{j+1}(\iota_{k}(s))

(24)

that send local
j
-strata at
s
to local
(j+1)
-strata at the unique
(k+1)
-stratum
\iota_{k}(s)
of
X
that intersects
s
.
The bijections in (
24
) are apparent in
Figure
1
.

3.3
Graded graphs from stratifications

A compact stratified
n
-manifold
X
defines a graded graph.
Its vertices are the strata of
X
, and the degree of a
k
-stratum
s
is
\deg(s)=\mathrm{codim}(s)=n-k
. Edges starting at a
k
-stratum
s
are local
(k+1)
-strata
q:s\to t
at
s
and connect
s
to the
(k+1)
-stratum
t
. The normals of
(n-1)
-strata equip the graph with normals.

Definition 3.22
.

Let
X
be a compact stratified
n
-manifold.
The
graded graph
associated to
X
is the regular graph
Q^{X}

with vertex set
Q^{X}_{0}
and edge set
Q^{X}_{1}
given by

Q^{X}_{0}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{n}S_{j}^{X},\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad Q^{X}_{1}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{n-1}\bigcup_{s\in S^{X}_{j}}S^{loc}_{j+1}(s).

The maps
s,t:Q_{1}^{X}\to Q_{0}^{X}
are given by
s(q)=u
,
t(q)=v
for a local
(k+1)
-stratum

q:u\to v
at
u\in S_{k}^{X}
.
The degree map

\deg:Q^{X}_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is given by
\deg(u)=\mathrm{codim}(u)=n-k
for
u\in S_{k}^{X}
. The normals of the
(n-1)
-strata of
X
define its normals.

Note that the saturation condition implies that each vertex
s\in Q^{X}_{0}
with
\deg(s)>0
has at least one outgoing edge. Also, each vertex
s\in Q^{X}_{0}
with
\deg(s)=1
has exactly two outgoing edges, so
Q^{X}
is indeed regular.
As the stratification of a
3
-manifold
X
induces the boundary stratification of
\partial X
, it also defines a graded graph
Q^{\partial X}
for
\partial X
.
Because the inclusions (
23
) preserve degrees and induce the bijections between the sets of local
j
-strata in (
24
), they define an insertion.

Lemma 3.23
.

For any homogeneous and saturated stratification of a compact
n
-manifold
X
, the maps from (
23
) and (
24
) define an insertion

i_{\partial}:Q^{\partial X}\to Q^{X}
,
and hence
I_{\partial}:\mathcal{Q}^{\partial X}\to\mathcal{Q}^{X}
is a discrete opfibration.

For a fine stratification of a compact
n
-manifold
X
the graph
Q^{X}
can be visualised by thickening the stratification.
This can be achieved for instance by choosing a triangulation that contains all skeleta as subcomplexes and taking the second barycentric subdivision.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

[formula]
[formula]

Figure 2:
Thickening
M_{th}
of a stratified 3-manifold
M
with vertices
u,v,w
, edges
d,e,f
and a plane
p
.

Definition 3.24
.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface with a fine stratification. The
thickened stratification

\Sigma_{th}
is the stratification obtained by thickening each edge of
\Sigma
to a rectangle and each vertex of
\Sigma
to an
n
-gon, where
n
is the number of local 1-strata at
v
. Its sets of planes, edges and vertices are given by

\displaystyle S^{th}_{k}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}\bigcup_{s\in S^{\Sigma}_{j}}S^{loc}_{j-k+2}(s),\qquad\textrm{ for }k=0,1,2.

The formula in
Definition
3.24
states that each plane of the thickening
\Sigma_{th}
corresponds to a unique vertex, edge or plane of
\Sigma
. We refer to them as red, green and white planes, respectively.

The edges of
\Sigma_{th}
are partitioned into two sets. Edges in the first set are called red edges and correspond to pairs of a vertex
v
of
\Sigma
and a local edge at
v
. Edges in the second set are called black edges and correspond to pairs of an edge
e
of
\Sigma
and a local plane at
e
.
Vertices of
\Sigma_{th}
are in bijection with pairs of a vertex
v
of
\Sigma
and a local plane at
v
. The vertex of
\Sigma_{th}
is contained in the associated plane of
\Sigma
.

Note that each red plane has only red edges, each green plane is a rectangle with two black and two red edges, and each white plane has only black edges.
Each black or red edge is in a unique green rectangle.
The orientation of the stratification of
\Sigma
induces an orientation of the red edges of
\Sigma_{th}
. They are oriented parallel to the normal of the associated edge.

Definition 3.25
.

Let
M
be a compact 3-manifold with a fine stratification. The
thickened stratification

M_{th}
is the stratification obtained by thickening each edge and plane to a polygonal cylinder and each vertex to a polyhedron. Its sets of regions, planes, edges and vertices are

\displaystyle S^{th}_{k}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}\bigcup_{s\in S^{M}_{j}}S^{loc}_{j-k+3}(s)\qquad k=0,1,2,3.

The formula in Definition
3.25
states that each region of the thickening corresponds to a unique region, plane, edge or vertex of
M
.
Planes of the thickening correspond to pairs (i)
of a vertex
v
of
M
and a local edge at
v
, (ii) of an edge
e
of
M
and a local plane at
e
and (iii) of a plane
p
of
M
and a local region at
p
. We refer to them as red, green and blue planes of
M_{th}
, respectively.

Edges of
M_{th}
correspond to pairs (i) of a vertex
v
of
M
and a local plane at
v
and (ii) of an edge
e
of
M
and a local region at
e
. We refer to them as red and black edges, respectively. Vertices of
M_{th}
correspond to pairs of a vertex
v
of
M
and a local region at
v
. The vertex of
M_{th}
is contained in the associated region of
M
.
Red planes contain only red edges, green planes are rectangles with two red and two black edges and blue planes contain only black edges.
Each black or red edge is contained in a unique green rectangle.

The orientation of the stratification of
M
defines an orientation for the edges of
M_{th}
. Red edges of
M_{th}
are oriented parallel to the normal of the associated plane. Black edges of
M_{th}
are oriented parallel to the orientation of the associated edge.

Note that a thickening of
M
defines a thickening
\partial M_{th}
of its boundary stratification such that the color conventions and orientations match. They are illustrated in Figure
2
.

X

X_{th}

Q^{X}

4
Defect cobordism category for untwisted Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT

In this section, we introduce the defect data for Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT
[
DW
]
with defects and the associated category of defect cobordisms. As Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT is a special case of Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury TQFT
[
TV
,
BW
]
, for a detailed discussion see Petit
[
P
]
, its defect data is a special case of the defect data for general Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury TQFTs
[
TV
,
BW
]
. Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury TQFT with defects was considered in
[
M
,
CM
]
and assigns the following data to the strata of a manifold of dimension
n=2,3

•

codim 0-strata: spherical fusion categories over
\mathbb{C}
,

•

codim 1-strata: finite semisimple
\mathbb{C}
-linear bimodule categories with bimodule traces,

•

codim 2-strata:
\mathbb{C}
-linear exact bimodule functors,

•

codim 3-strata: bimodule natural transformations.

Here, the spherical fusion categories acting on the bimodule category for a codim 1-stratum are the ones of its local codim 0-strata.
The bimodule functor for a codim 2-stratum relates the bimodule categories of its local codim 1-strata, and the bimodule natural transformation for a codim 3-stratum relates the bimodule functors of its local codim 2-strata. The bimodule traces, introduced by Schaumann
[
S13
,
S15
]
and developed further by Fuchs, Schweigert and Schaumann
[
FSS
]
, are required for the state sum formulation of the TQFT.

Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT without defects amounts to specialising the spherical fusion categories to the spherical fusion categories
\mathrm{Vec}^{\omega}_{G}
of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces graded by a finite group
G

and linear maps that preserve the grading. The monoidal structure is given by the 3-cocycle
\omega:G\times G\times G\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
with the trivial cocycle
\omega\equiv 1
for the untwisted case. For
\omega\equiv 1
,
\mathrm{Vec}_{G}
is has a spherical structure induced by the duals in
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
, see
[
P
]
.

As shown in
[
EGNO
]
, Example 7.4.10 and Exercise 7.4.11, finite-dimensional module categories over
\mathrm{Vec}_{G}
are given by finite
G
-sets and certain 2-cocycles. We only consider trivial 2-cocycles in the following. In this case, a finite semisimple
(\mathrm{Vec}_{G},\mathrm{Vec}_{H})
-bimodule category
\mathcal{M}
corresponds to a finite
G\times H^{op}
-set
M
, whose elements are the simple objects of
\mathcal{M}
.
Bimodule functors and natural transformations between such bimodule categories can be analysed by considering the images
and coherence isomorphisms on simple objects. It is shown in
[
L
, Example 5.11]
that bimodule functors
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
between
(\mathrm{Vec}_{G},\mathrm{Vec}_{H})
-bimodule categories
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{N}
correspond to representations
\rho:(M\times N)\sslash(G\times H)\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
of the associated action groupoid and bimodule natural transformations
\eta:F\Rightarrow F^{\prime}
between such functors to intertwiners of such representations.

In the following we do not consider Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury TQFT with or without defects beyond the Dijkgraaf-Witten case and also do not consider twisting by cocycles. We thus assign the following defect data:

•

codim 0-strata: finite groups,

•

codim 1-strata: finite sets with group actions,

•

codim 2-strata: representations of action groupoids,

•

codim 3-strata: intertwiners of representations of action groupoids.

For lack of a better terminology, the first two layers of data are called
classical
defect data, because they are given by groups and group actions, the last two
quantum
defect data, because they are linear.

We start by considering the classical defect data.
The groups acting on the finite set assigned to a codim 1-stratum are specified by its normals from
Definition
3.9
. In the associated graded graph
Q^{X}
this corresponds to the edges
l(s):s\to L(s)
and
r(s):s\to R(s)
from Definition
3.5
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a compact stratified
n
-manifold.
An
assignment of classical defect data
to
X

•

assigns to each
n
-stratum
t
a finite group
G_{t}
,

•

assigns to each
(n-1)
-stratum
s
a finite
G_{L(s)}\times G_{R(s)}^{op}
-set
M_{s}
.

Note that an assignment of classical defect data to a compact stratified
n
-manifold
X
defines an assignment of classical defect data to its boundary
\partial X
.

We now show it assigns to each stratum
r
of
X
an action groupoid
\mathcal{D}_{r}=M_{r}\sslash G_{r}
and to each local stratum
p:u\to v
a functor
D_{p}:\mathcal{D}_{u}\to\mathcal{D}_{v}
.

Action groupoids for strata:

The set
M_{r}
in the groupoid

\mathcal{D}_{r}=M_{r}\sslash G_{r}
is the product of the sets
M_{s}
for all local
(n-1)
-strata
x:r\to s
. The group
G_{r}
is the product of the groups
G_{t}
for all local
n
-strata
q:r\to t

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{r}:=M_{r}\sslash G_{r}=\left(\textstyle\prod_{S^{loc}_{n-1}(r)\ni x:r\to s}M_{s}\right){\sslash}\left(\textstyle\prod_{S^{loc}_{n}(r)\ni q:r\to t}G_{t}\right).

(25)

The action of
G_{r}
on
M_{r}
is given by the commuting diagram

\textstyle{\left(\prod_{S^{loc}_{n}(r)\ni q:r\to t}G_{t}\right)\times\left(\prod_{S^{loc}_{n-1}(r)\ni x:r\to s}M_{s}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\rhd}
\scriptstyle{\langle\pi_{lx},\pi_{rx},\pi_{x}\rangle}
\textstyle{\left(\prod_{S^{loc}_{n-1}(r)\ni x:r\to s}M_{s}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{x}}
\textstyle{G_{L(s)}\times G_{R(s)}\times M_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt(g_{L},g_{R},m)\mapsto g_{L}\rhd m\lhd g_{R}^{-1}}
\textstyle{M_{s},}

where
lx=S_{n}^{x}(l(s))
and
rx=S_{n}^{x}(r(s))
are given by the map
S_{n}^{x}:S^{loc}_{n}(s)=\{l(s),r(s)\}\to S^{loc}_{n}(r)
from Lemma
3.17
for the local
(n-1)
-stratum
x:r\to s
.

In particular,
\mathcal{D}_{t}=\bullet\sslash G_{t}
for each
n
-stratum
t
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}=M_{s}\sslash G_{L(s)}\times G_{R(s)}
for each
(n-1)
-stratum
s
. For an
(n-1)
-stratum
s
that occurs in several local
(n-1)
-strata
q:r\to s
, the associated set
M_{s}
appears several times as a factor in
M_{r}
. Likewise, if
an
n
-stratum
t
corresponds to several local
n
-strata
q:r\to t
, the group
G_{t}
appears several times in
G_{r}
.

Example 4.2
.

For the stratification in Example
3.16
the action groupoid associated to the
0
-stratum
x
is

\mathcal{D}_{x}=M_{x}\sslash G_{x}=(M_{t}^{\times 2}\times M_{u}^{\times 2}\times M_{v}^{\times 2}\times M_{w}^{\times 2})\sslash(G_{S}^{\times 3}\times G_{T}\times G_{U}^{\times 2}\times G_{V}\times G_{W}).

The factors in the set
M_{x}
are in bijection with the local 1-strata at
x
and the factors in the group
G_{x}
with the local 2-strata at
x
. If
we index elements by the associated local strata, the action for
\mathcal{D}_{x}
reads

\displaystyle m_{t_{1}}\mapsto g_{T}\rhd m_{t_{1}}\lhd g_{U_{1}}^{-1}

\displaystyle m_{t_{2}}\mapsto g_{T}\rhd m_{t_{2}}\lhd g_{U_{2}}^{-1}

\displaystyle m_{u_{1}}\mapsto g_{U_{1}}\rhd m_{u_{1}}\lhd g_{S_{3}}^{-1}

\displaystyle m_{u_{2}}\mapsto g_{U_{2}}\rhd m_{u_{2}}\lhd g_{S_{1}}^{-1}

\displaystyle m_{v_{1}}\mapsto g_{S_{1}}\rhd m_{v_{1}}\lhd g_{V}^{-1}

\displaystyle m_{v_{2}}\mapsto g_{S_{2}}\rhd m_{v_{2}}\lhd g_{V}^{-1}

\displaystyle m_{w_{1}}\mapsto g_{S_{2}}\rhd m_{w_{1}}\lhd g_{W}^{-1}

\displaystyle g_{w_{2}}\mapsto g_{S_{3}}\rhd m_{w_{2}}\lhd g_{W}^{-1}.

Functors for local strata:

For a local
l
-stratum
p:u\to v
, the maps

S_{n}^{p}:S^{loc}_{n}(v)\to S^{loc}_{n}(u)\qquad S_{n-1}^{p}:S^{loc}_{n-1}(v)\to S^{loc}_{n-1}(u)

from Lemma

assign

a local

and to each local

a local
(n-1)
-stratum

.
This defines

f_{p}:M_{u}\to M_{v}
and
a group homomorphism
\phi_{p}:G_{u}\to G_{v}
, given by the commuting diagrams

\textstyle{\displaystyle\prod_{S_{n-1}^{loc}(u)\ni x^{\prime}:u\to s}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!M_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{x^{p}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{p}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\prod_{S_{n-1}^{loc}(v)\ni x:v\to s}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!M_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{x}}
\textstyle{M_{s}}

\textstyle{\displaystyle\prod_{S_{n}^{loc}(u)\ni q^{\prime}:u\to t}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!G_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{q^{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{p}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\prod_{S_{n}^{loc}(v)\ni q:v\to t}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!G_{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{q}}
\textstyle{G_{t}}

for all local
(n-1)
-strata
x:v\to s
and
n
-strata

.
As the maps from Lemma

satisfy

\displaystyle S_{n}^{p}\circ S^{x}_{n-1}=S_{n}^{x^{p}}:S_{n}^{loc}(s)\to S_{n}^{loc}(u)

(38)

for all local
(n-1)
-strata

,
and by definition of the action in (

) the
diagram

\textstyle{G_{u}\times M_{u}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\phi_{p},f_{p}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\pi_{l(x^{p})},\pi_{r(x^{p})},\pi_{x^{p}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\rhd}
\textstyle{G_{v}\times M_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rhd}
\scriptstyle{\langle\pi_{lx},\pi_{rx},\pi_{x}\rangle\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{G_{L(s)}\times G_{R(s)}\times M_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rhd}
\textstyle{M_{s}}
\textstyle{M_{u}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{x^{p}}}
\textstyle{M_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{x}}

commutes for all local
l
-strata
p:u\to v
, where
l(x^{p})=S_{n}^{x^{p}}(l(s))
,
r(x^{p})=S_{n}^{x^{p}}(r(s))
,
lx=S_{n}^{x}(l(s))
and

. The lower triangle commutes by (
4
), the quadrilaterals by (

) and the upper triangle by definition of
\phi_{p}
and

in (

) and due to identity (

), which implies
l(x^{p})=(lx)^{p}
and
