We now compute the vector space
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)
of the defect TQFT for different stratifications of surfaces. The first example shows that for a surface with transparent defects the result is the ground state of the quantum double model or, equivalently, the vector space assigned by a Dijkgraaf-Witten TQFT.

Example 7.5
.

(Ground state of Kitaev’s quantum double model)

Stratification and defect data:

Let
\Sigma
be a surface with a fine stratification and dual graph
\Gamma
, such that

•

every vertex
v
of
\Gamma
is labelled with the same finite group
G
,

•

every edge
e
of
\Gamma
is labelled with
G
as an
G\times G^{op}
-set,

•

every
n
-valent plaquette
p
of
\Gamma
is labelled with
\rho_{p}:G^{\times n}\sslash G^{\times n}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}

that assigns the vector space
\mathbb{C}
if the ordered oriented product of the group elements at edges of
p
is trivial and else
0
.

Gauge configurations and gauge transformations:

The groupoid of gauge configurations and of gauge transformations is

\mathcal{A}\sslash\mathcal{G}=G^{\times E}\sslash G^{\times V}.

•

A gauge configuration is an assignment of an element
g_{e}\in G
to each edge
e
of
\Gamma
.

•

A gauge transformation is an assignment of an element
g_{v}\in G
to each vertex
v
of
\Gamma
and acts by

g_{e}\mapsto g_{t(e)}\cdot g_{e}\cdot g_{s(e)}^{-1}.

Vector space for the defect surface:

The functor
F_{\Sigma}:\mathcal{A}\sslash\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
assigns to a gauge configuration the null vector space unless
the ordered oriented product of the group elements vanishes
at all plaquettes and in that case the vector space
\mathbb{C}
.
It follows that
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)=\lim F_{\Sigma}
is given by

\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)=\langle\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\sslash\mathcal{G})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}=\langle\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),G)/G\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

We now consider an example where all edges of
\Gamma
are labelled again by a single group
G
, but where some plaquettes are equipped with non-trivial representations of the associated action groupoids. This corresponds to quantum double models with excitations that are treated in
[
BK2
]
and
[
KK
]
.

Example 7.6
.

(Excitations in quantum double models)

Stratification:

Let
\Sigma
be an oriented surface of genus
g
with marked points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and
\Sigma^{*}=\Sigma\setminus\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
. Choose a directed graph
\Gamma
consisting of loops
m_{i}

that go counterclockwise around
x_{i}

and
a
- and
b
-cycles
a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{g},b_{g}
, all based at
x\in\Sigma^{*}
, such that

\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},x)=\langle a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{g},b_{g},m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}\mid m_{1}\cdots m_{n}[a_{1},b_{1}]\cdots[a_{g},b_{g}]=1\rangle.

Then
\Gamma
has
n
plaquettes
p_{i}
bordered by the loops
m_{i}
and a plaquette
p
bordered by
m=m_{1}\cdots m_{n}[a_{1},b_{1}]\cdots[a_{g},b_{g}]
.

Defect data:

We assign

•

to each edge
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n},a_{1},\ldots,b_{g}
a copy of the same finite group
G
,

•

to the plaquette
p_{i}
a representation
\rho_{i}:G\sslash G\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
as in Corollary
7.3
, given by
a conjugacy class
\mathcal{C}_{i}\subset G
and a representation
\sigma_{i}:N_{i}\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{i})
of
N_{i}=\mathrm{Stab}(g_{i})
for some
g_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
,

•

to the plaquette
p
the representation
\rho:G\sslash G\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
given by the conjugacy class
\{1\}\subset G
and the trivial representation
\sigma:G\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C})
.

Gauge configurations and gauge transformations:

The groupoid of reduced gauge configurations and gauge transformations is given as follows:

•

A gauge configuration is an assignment of an element of
G
to each loop
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n},a_{1},\ldots,b_{g}
.

•

The group of gauge transformations is
G
and acts by simultaneous conjugation.

Vector space of the defect surface:

The functor
F_{\Sigma}:\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{\Sigma}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
assigns to a gauge configuration
A
the null vector space, unless the group elements for
m_{i}
and
m
are all in the assigned conjugacy classes. In this case, one has
F_{\Sigma}(A)=V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}
.

Thus, gauge configurations that contribute to
\lim F_{\Sigma}
are in bijection with elements of the set

\mathrm{Hom}_{*}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},x),G)
of group homomorphisms
f:\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},x)\to G
with
f(m_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
.
With formula (
11
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)=\lim F_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bigoplus_{A\in\mathcal{R}(\mathrm{Hom}_{*}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},x),G))}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n})^{\mathrm{Aut}(A)}\cong(V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[G]}\langle\mathrm{Hom}_{*}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},x),G)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}},

where the direct sum runs over a set of representatives of the conjugacy classes in
\mathrm{Hom}_{*}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,x),G)
and the actions of
\mathbb{C}[G]
are induced by the conjugation action of
G
and by Corollary
7.3
. This coincides with the result of
[
BK2
]
and
[
KK
]
if one restricts their results to representation categories of finite groups.

In the next example we compute the vector space
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)
in the presence of a domain wall and a domain wall excitation. Here, it is more efficient to work with a coherent basepoint set.

Example 7.7
.

(Domain walls and excitations on domain walls)

Stratification:

Let
\Sigma
be a surface of genus
g
,
e
an oriented separating curve on
\Sigma
such that
\Sigma\setminus e=\Omega_{1}\amalg\Omega_{2}
and
v
a vertex on
e
, as in Figure
5
.
Consider the stratification of
\Sigma
with

•

2-strata
t_{i}=\Omega_{i}
with
\hat{t}_{i}=\overline{\Omega}_{i}
,

•

1-stratum
s=e\setminus v
with
\hat{s}=[0,1]
,

•

0-stratum
v
with
\hat{v}=\{v\}
,

that corresponds to the graded graph

v
s
t_{2}
t_{1}
\iota^{1}_{v}
\iota^{0}_{v}
\iota_{2}
\iota_{1}

The set
\{v\}
defines a coherent basepoint set. The maps
\iota^{i}_{v}:\hat{v}\to\hat{s}
,
v\mapsto i
send
v
to the endpoints of
[0,1]
.
The maps
\iota_{i}:\hat{s}\to\hat{t}_{i}
from (
5.1
) send both endpoints of
[0,1]
to
v\in\overline{\Omega}_{i}
.

Defect data:

Orient the normal of
e
such that it points towards
\Omega_{1}
and assign:

•

to the 2-strata
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
finite groups
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,

•

to the 1-stratum
s
a finite
G_{1}\times G_{2}^{op}
-set
M
,

•

to the vertex
v
a representation
\rho:M\times M\sslash G_{1}\times G_{2}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
given by a
G_{1}\times G_{2}
-orbit
\mathcal{O}\subset M\times M
and a representation
\sigma:N\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)
of a stabiliser.

Gauge configurations and gauge transformations:

The groupoid of reduced gauge configurations and gauge transformations on
\Sigma
is given as follows:

•

a gauge configuration by group homomorphisms
\rho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\overline{\Omega}_{i},v)\to G_{i}
and
m_{s},m_{t}\in M
such that

\displaystyle m_{t}=\rho_{1}(e)\rhd m_{s}\lhd\rho_{2}(e)^{-1},

(172)

•

a gauge transformation by a pair
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G_{1}\times G_{2}
acting on a gauge configuration as

\displaystyle\rho_{i}\mapsto g_{i}\cdot\rho_{i}\cdot g_{i}^{-1},\qquad m_{s}\mapsto g_{1}\rhd m_{s}\lhd g_{2}^{-1},\qquad m_{t}\mapsto g_{1}\rhd m_{t}\lhd g_{2}^{-1}.

(173)

As
m_{t}
is determined by
m_{s}
and
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
via (
172
), we can omit
m_{t}
and set
m=m_{s}
.

Vector space of the defect surface:

The functor
F_{\Sigma}:\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{\Sigma}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
assigns to a gauge configuration
A=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},m)
the vector space

V
if
m\in M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
and else the null vector space, where

M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}=\{m\in M\mid(m,\rho_{1}(e)\rhd m\lhd\rho_{2}(e)^{-1})\in\mathcal{O}\}.

We denote by

\mathcal{R}
a set of representatives of the orbits of the
G_{1}\times G_{2}
-action (
173
) on the set of triples
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},m)
with
m\in M_{(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}
.
Then
Formula
(
11
) yields the vector space

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)=\lim F_{\Sigma}=\!\!\!\!\bigoplus_{(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},m)\in\mathcal{R}}\!\!\!\!V^{\mathrm{Aut}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},m)}.

Transparent point defect:

If
\rho:M\times M\sslash G\times H\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the transparent representation with
\rho(m,m)=\mathbb{C}
and
\rho(m,m^{\prime})=0
for
m\neq m^{\prime}
, then
M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}=M^{\pi_{1}(e,v)}
is the fixed point set of the
\mathbb{Z}=\pi_{1}(e,v)
-action

\rhd:\mathbb{Z}\times M\to M,\qquad n\rhd m=\rho_{1}(e)^{n}\rhd m\lhd\rho_{2}(e)^{-n}.

Denoting by
\mathcal{A}^{*}
the the set of triples
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},m)
of group homomorphisms
\rho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\overline{\Omega}_{i},v)\to G_{i}
and a fixed point
m\in M^{\pi_{1}(e,v)}
and by

\sim
the equivalence relation given by (
173
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)=\lim F_{\Sigma}=\langle\mathcal{A}^{*}/\sim\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

Transparent point and line defect:

For
G_{1}=G_{2}=M=G
the condition
\rho_{1}(e)\rhd m\lhd\rho_{2}(e)^{-1}=m
states that
\rho_{1}(e),\rho_{2}(e)\in G
are conjugate. By applying the gauge transformations (
173
) one can achieve
\rho_{1}(e)=\rho_{2}(e)
and combine
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
into a group homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to G
.
Then
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)
is the ground state from Example
7.5
.

Impermeable line defect:

If
M=\bullet
and
v
carries the transparent representation, then
gauge configurations are given by group homomorphisms
\rho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\overline{\Omega}_{i},v)\to G_{i}
for
i=1,2
and gauge transformations act as in (
173
). This yields

\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)=\lim F_{\Sigma}=\langle\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\overline{\Omega}_{1},v),G_{1})/G_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\langle\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\overline{\Omega}_{2},v),G_{2})/G_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

\Omega_{2}
\Omega_{1}
e
v

Figure 5:
Separating curve
e
on a surface
\Sigma
with
\Sigma\setminus e=\Omega_{1}\amalg\Omega_{2}
and a vertex
v
on
e
.

7.3
Defect cobordisms

In this section we compute examples of the linear maps associated with defect cobordisms
\Sigma_{0}\xrightarrow{\iota_{0}}M\xleftarrow{\iota_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
. The simplest case is a closed 3-manifold
M
with
\Sigma_{0}=\Sigma_{1}=\emptyset
. In this case one has
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{0})=\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{1})=\mathbb{C}
and the linear map
\mathcal{Z}(M):\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
is a complex number that depends on the manifold
M
as well as the defect data of the defect strata inside it.

Example 7.8
.

(Closed 3-manifolds with defects)

Let
M
be closed stratified 3-manifold, viewed as a defect cobordism.
Then
\mathcal{A}^{\partial M}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{\partial M}=\mathcal{A}^{\emptyset}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{\emptyset}=\bullet
is the terminal groupoid, and the
functor from (
153
)
for the boundary is
F_{\partial M}=F_{\emptyset}=\mathbb{C}:\bullet\to\mathbb{C}
.

The natural transformation
\mu^{M}:\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{C}

from (
160
) and Proposition
6.7
is a map
\mu^{M}:\mathcal{A}^{M}\to\mathbb{C}
,
A\mapsto\mu^{M}_{A}
that assigns to every gauge configuration a number in
\mathbb{C}
and is constant on the gauge equivalence classes.
The functor
\mathcal{Z}:\mathrm{Cob}^{\mathrm{def}}_{3}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from Theorem
6.8
averages
\mu^{M}
over the gauge equivalence classes of gauge configurations on
M
.
Proposition
2.14
and the orbit stabiliser theorem yield

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(M)=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{A\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A}^{M}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{M})}\!\frac{\mu^{M}_{A}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(A)|}=|\mathcal{G}_{M}|^{-1}\!\!\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}^{M}}\mu^{M}_{A}.

(174)

If
M
has only 2- and 3-strata, then
\mu^{M}:\mathcal{A}^{M}\to\mathbb{C}
is constant, and
\mathcal{Z}(M)
counts the number of gauge equivalence classes of gauge configurations on
M
, weighted by the cardinality of their automorphism groups.

A specific example of a closed 3-manifold with only 2- and 3-strata is one obtained by gluing two handlebodies along a genus
g
-defect surface. This yields a simple expression for the associated manifold invariant.

Example 7.9
.

(Closed 3-manifold by gluing handlebodies)

Stratification:

Let
M
be a 3-manifold obtained by gluing two handlebodies
H_{1},H_{2}
of genus
g
along their boundaries
\partial H_{1}=\partial H_{2}=\Sigma
.
This defines a stratification of
M
with

•

two 3-strata
t_{i}=\mathring{H}_{i}
with associated spaces
\hat{t}_{i}=H_{i}
,

•

a single 2-stratum
s=\Sigma
with
\hat{s}=s=\Sigma
,

•

the maps
\iota_{i}:\hat{s}\to\hat{t}_{i}
from (
5.1
) given by the inclusions
\iota_{i}:\Sigma\to H_{i}
.

Any point
x\in\Sigma
defines a coherent basepoint set and group homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(\iota_{i}):\pi_{1}(\Sigma,x)\to\pi_{1}(H_{i},x)
.

Defect data:

Let the normal of
\Sigma
point towards
H_{1}
and label

•

the 3-strata
\mathring{H}_{i}
with finite groups
G_{i}
,

•

the 2-stratum
\Sigma
with a finite
G_{1}\times G_{2}^{op}
-set
N
.

Gauge configurations and gauge transformations:

The groupoid of reduced gauge configurations and transformations with respect to
\{x\}
is given as follows:

•

A gauge configuration is a triple
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},n)
of
group homomorphisms
\rho_{i}:\pi_{1}(H_{i},x)\to G_{i}
and a fixed point
n\in N
under the group action

\displaystyle\rhd:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,x)\times N\to N,\qquad(\lambda,n)\mapsto\rho_{1}\circ\pi_{1}(\iota_{1})(\lambda)\rhd n\lhd\rho_{2}\circ\pi_{1}(\iota_{2})(\lambda)^{-1}.

(175)

•

A gauge transformation is an element
g=(g_{1},g_{2})\in G_{1}\times G_{2}
and acts by

\displaystyle\rho_{i}\mapsto g_{i}\cdot\rho_{i}\cdot g_{i}^{-1},\qquad n\mapsto g_{1}\rhd n\lhd g_{2}^{-1}.

(176)

Linear map for the defect cobordism:

As
\pi_{1}(H_{i},x)\cong F_{g}
, conjugacy classes of group homomorphisms
\rho_{i}:\pi_{1}(H_{i},x)\to G_{i}
are simply conjugacy classes
C_{i}\subset G_{i}^{\times g}
under simultaneous conjugation. Denoting by
N^{(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}
the fixed point set of (
175
), we obtain with
Proposition
2.14
and the orbit stabiliser theorem

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(M)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},n)]}|\mathrm{Aut}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},n)|^{-1}=\frac{1}{|G_{1}|\cdot|G_{2}|}\sum_{\rho_{1}\in G_{1}^{\times g}}\sum_{\rho_{2}\in G_{2}^{\times g}}|N^{(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}|.

(177)

Transparent defect surface:

If
G_{1}=G_{2}=N=G
with
g_{1}\rhd m\lhd g_{2}^{-1}=g_{1}\cdot m\cdot g_{2}^{-1}
, then (
175
) states that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
induce conjugate group homomorphisms
\rho_{i}\circ\pi_{1}(\iota_{i}):\pi_{1}(\Sigma,x)\to G
and hence define a group homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(M,x)\to G
. Formula (
177
) then reduces to the formula for untwisted Dijkgraaf-Witten theory without defects

\mathcal{Z}(M)=|G|^{-1}\cdot\left|\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\right|.

Impermeable defect surface:

For the terminal set
N=\bullet
formula (
177
) reduces to

\mathcal{Z}(M)=|G_{1}|^{g-1}\cdot|G_{2}|^{g-1}=\mathcal{Z}(H_{1})\cdot\mathcal{Z}(H_{2})
.

We now consider an example of a 3-manifold
X
with a non-trivial boundary
\partial X
, viewed as a defect cobordism
\partial X\xrightarrow{\iota}X\leftarrow\emptyset
.
In particular, it shows how defect lines on transparent defect planes are associated with representation of the Drinfeld double
D(G)
.

Example 7.10
.

(Solid torus with two defect planes and an isolated loop)

We consider the defect cobordism
X=D^{2}\times S^{1}
with
\partial X=S^{1}\times S^{1}

obtained by gluing the top and bottom face of the following cylinder along the identity map

s_{2}
s_{1}
r
t_{1}
t_{2}
q_{3}
q_{3}
q_{1}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{2}

Stratification:
The stratification has a single 1-stratum
r
, two 2-strata
s_{1},s_{2}
and two 3-strata
t_{1},t_{2}
. Its boundary
1-strata are
u_{1}=s_{1}\cap\partial X
,
u_{2}=s_{2}\cap\partial X
and its boundary 2-strata
v_{1}=t_{1}\cap\partial X
,
v_{2}=t_{2}\cap\partial X
.
The spaces associated to the strata and boundary strata of
X
are given by

•

r=\hat{r}=S^{1}
is a circle,

•

\hat{s}_{1}=\hat{s}_{2}=[0,1]\times S^{1}
are annuli,

•

\hat{t}_{1}=\hat{t}_{2}=D^{2}_{+}\times S^{1}
, where
D_{+}^{2}
is a closed half-disc,

•

u_{1}=\hat{u}_{1}=u_{2}=\hat{u}_{2}=S^{1}
are circles,

•

\hat{v}_{1}=\hat{v}_{2}=[0,1]\times S^{1}
are annuli.

The point
q_{3}\in r
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in\partial X
define a coherent basepoint set for
X
.

Classical defect data:
The strata of
X
are labelled as follows

•

the 3-strata
t_{1},t_{2}
with finite groups
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,

•

the 2-strata
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
with finite
G_{1}\times G_{2}^{op}
-sets
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
,

•

the 1-stratum
r
with a representation
\phi:M_{1}\times M_{2}\sslash G_{1}\times G_{2}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Gauge configurations and gauge transformations:

A gauge configuration for the coherent basepoint set consists of

•

functors
\rho_{j}:\Pi_{1}(\hat{t}_{j},\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\})\to\bullet\sslash G_{j}
for
j=1,2
,

•

elements
m_{1}\in M_{1}
,
m_{2}\in M_{2}
for
q_{1},q_{2}
and
m^{\prime}_{1}\in M_{1}
,
m^{\prime}_{2}\in M_{2}
for
q_{3}
that satisfy

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(\sigma_{1})\rhd m_{1}\lhd\rho_{2}(\sigma_{1})^{-1}=m_{1},

\displaystyle\rho_{2}(\sigma_{2})\rhd m_{2}\lhd\rho_{1}(\sigma_{2})^{-1}=m_{2},

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(\tau_{1})\rhd m_{1}\lhd\rho_{2}(\tau_{1})^{-1}=m^{\prime}_{1},

\displaystyle\rho_{2}(\tau_{2})\rhd m_{2}\lhd\rho_{1}(\tau_{2})^{-1}=m^{\prime}_{2},

for all paths
\sigma_{j}:q_{j}\to q_{j}
and
\tau_{j}:q_{j}\to q_{3}
in
\hat{s}_{j}
for
j=1,2
.

Gauge transformations are elements
(h_{1}^{1},h_{1}^{2},h^{3}_{1},h_{2}^{1},h_{2}^{2},h^{3}_{2})\in G^{\times 3}_{1}\times G^{\times 3}_{2}
, where
h^{j}_{1}\in G_{1}
and
h^{j}_{2}\in G_{2}
are associated to
q_{j}
for
j=1,2,3
. They act by
\rho_{i}(\nu)\mapsto h_{i}^{k}\cdot\rho_{i}(\nu)\cdot(h_{i}^{l})^{-1}
for all paths
\nu:q_{l}\to q_{k}
in
\hat{t}_{i}
and by

\displaystyle m_{1}\mapsto h_{1}^{1}\rhd m_{1}\lhd(h_{2}^{1})^{-1},\quad m_{2}\mapsto h_{2}^{2}\rhd m_{2}\lhd(h_{1}^{2})^{-1},\quad m^{\prime}_{1}\mapsto h_{1}^{3}\rhd m^{\prime}_{1}\lhd(h_{2}^{3})^{-1},\quad m^{\prime}_{2}\mapsto h_{2}^{3}\rhd m^{\prime}_{2}\lhd(h_{1}^{3})^{-1}.

Reduced gauge groupoid:

We fix paths
\tau_{j}:q_{j}\to q_{3}
in
\hat{s}_{j}
for
j=1,2
. By applying gauge transformations
(h_{1}^{1},1,1,h_{2}^{1},1,1)
at
q_{1}
and
(1,h_{1}^{2},1,1,h_{2}^{2},1)
at
q_{2}
we can achieve
\rho_{1}(\tau_{j})=\rho_{2}(\tau_{j})=1
for
j=1,2
, which implies
m^{\prime}_{1}=m_{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}=m_{2}
.

This yields a reduced gauge groupoid
\mathcal{A}^{X}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{X}=\mathcal{A}^{\partial X}\sslash G^{\partial X}
, where the projection functor on the boundary gauge groupoid is the identity
P_{\partial}=\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{A}^{X}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{X}\to\mathcal{A}^{\partial X}\sslash\mathcal{G}^{\partial X}
, and whose

•

gauge configurations are quadruples
(g_{1},g_{2},m_{1},m_{2})
with
g_{j}=\rho_{j}(\sigma_{1})\in G_{j}
,
m_{j}\in M_{j}
such that

\displaystyle g_{1}\rhd m_{1}\lhd g_{2}^{-1}=m_{1}\qquad g_{2}\rhd m_{2}\lhd g_{1}^{-1}=m_{2},

(178)

•

gauge transformations are elements
(h_{1},h_{2})\in G_{1}\times G_{2}
and act by

\displaystyle m_{1}\mapsto h_{1}\rhd m_{1}\lhd h_{2}^{-1},\qquad m_{2}\mapsto h_{2}\rhd m_{2}\lhd h_{1}^{-1},\qquad g_{1}\mapsto h_{1}g_{1}h_{1}^{-1},\qquad g_{2}\mapsto h_{2}g_{2}h_{2}^{-1}.

(179)

Boundary vector space:

The functor
F_{\partial X}:\mathcal{A}^{\partial X}\sslash G^{\partial X}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
from (
153
) is the constant functor
F_{\partial X}=\mathbb{C}
that assigns to every boundary gauge configuration the vector space
\mathbb{C}
and to every boundary gauge transformation
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
The defect TQFT assigns to
\partial X
the free complex vector space generated by the path-components of
\mathcal{A}^{\partial X}\sslash G^{\partial X}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)=\lim F_{\partial X}

\displaystyle=\langle\,\{(g_{1},g_{2},m_{1},m_{2})\in G_{1}\times G_{2}\times M_{1}\times M_{2}\mid g_{1}\rhd m_{1}\lhd g^{-1}_{2}=m_{1},g_{2}\rhd m_{2}\lhd g^{-1}_{1}=m_{2}\}\,/\,G_{1}\times G_{2}\,\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

Quantum defect data:

We interpret
X
as a cobordism
\partial X\xrightarrow{\iota}X\leftarrow\emptyset
. The
natural transformation
\mu^{X}:F_{\partial X}=\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{C}
from (
160
) is given by the representation
\phi:M_{1}\times M_{2}\sslash G_{1}\times G_{2}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
for the isolated loop
r

and has components

\mu^{X}_{(g_{1},g_{2},m_{1},m_{2})}=\mathrm{tr}(\phi_{m_{1},m_{2}}(g_{1},g_{2}))\in\mathbb{C},

where

\phi_{m_{1},m_{2}}(g_{1},g_{2}):\phi(m_{1},m_{2})\to\phi(m_{1},m_{2})

is the linear endomorphism assigned to the automorphism
(g_{1},g_{2}):(m_{1},m_{2})\to(m_{1},m_{2})
in
M_{1}\times M_{2}\sslash G_{1}\times G_{2}
.
The linear map
\mathcal{Z}(X):\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)\to\mathbb{C}
for the cobordism
\partial X\xrightarrow{\iota}X\leftarrow\emptyset
is the image of
\mu^{X}:\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{C}
under the functor
L
from Proposition
2.14
and given by

\mathcal{Z}(X):\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)\to\mathbb{C},\quad[(g_{1},g_{2},m_{1},m_{2})]\mapsto\mu^{X}_{(g_{1},g_{2},m_{1},m_{2})}.

Transparent defect line:

If we choose
M_{1}=M_{2}=M
and the transparent representation
\phi:M\times M\sslash G_{1}\times G_{2}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
with
\phi(m_{1},m_{2})=\delta_{m_{1}}(m_{2})\mathbb{C}
, then we have
\mu^{X}_{(g_{1},g_{2},m_{1},m_{2})}=\delta_{m_{1}}(m_{2})
and

\mathcal{Z}(X):\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)\to\mathbb{C},\quad[(g_{1},g_{2},m_{1},m_{2})]\mapsto\delta_{m_{1}}(m_{2}).

Transparent defect surfaces:

If we choose two transparent defect surfaces, we have

G_{1}=G_{2}=M_{1}=M_{2}=G
.
Condition (
178
) states that

g_{2}=m_{2}\cdot g_{1}\cdot m_{2}^{-1}\qquad g_{1}=(m_{1}m_{2})\cdot g_{1}\cdot(m_{1}m_{2})^{-1}.

By applying a gauge transformation
h_{2}\in G
, we can achieve
m_{2}=1
and
g_{2}=g_{1}
.
By setting
a:=m_{1}\in G
and
b:=g_{1}\in G
we transform the second condition into
[a,b]=1
and obtain the reduced gauge groupoid

\mathcal{A}^{X}\sslash G^{X}=\mathcal{A}^{\partial X}\sslash G^{\partial X}=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z},G)/G.

The representation
\phi:G\times G\sslash G\times G\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
is then given by a representation
\phi^{\prime}:G\sslash G\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
of the action groupoid for the conjugation action of
G
on itself. By Example
7.4
this corresponds to a representation of the Drinfeld double
D(G)
. If we choose an irreducible representation as in Corollary
7.3
and Example
7.4
given by a conjugacy class
\mathcal{C}\subset G
and a representation
\sigma:N\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)
we obtain

\mathcal{Z}(X):\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)\to\mathbb{C},\quad[(a,b)]\mapsto\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(a)\,\mathrm{tr}\rho_{\sigma}(b).

In particular, a transparent defect line
r
corresponds to
\mathcal{C}=\{1\}
and the trivial representation
\sigma
. In this case, the linear map associated to
X
coincides with the one of Dijkgraaf-Witten theory for
G
without defects

\mathcal{Z}(X)=\mathcal{Z}_{DW}:\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)\to\mathbb{C},\quad[(a,b)]\mapsto\delta_{e}(a).

Impermeable defect surfaces:

If we choose sets
M_{1},M_{2}
with the trivial
G_{1}\times G_{2}
-actions, a representation
\phi:M_{1}\times M_{2}\sslash G_{1}\times G_{2}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
is simply an assignment of a complex vector space
\phi(m_{1},m_{2})
to each element
(m_{1},m_{2})\in M_{1}\times M_{2}
. Condition (
178
) is trivially satisfied and the boundary vector space is given by the
sets
C_{G_{i}}
of conjugacy classes in
G_{i}

\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)=\langle C_{G_{1}}\times C_{G_{2}}\times M_{1}\times M_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

The linear map for the cobordism becomes

\mathcal{Z}(X):\mathcal{Z}(\partial X)\to\mathbb{C},\quad(C_{g_{1}},C_{g_{2}},m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\phi(m_{1},m_{2}).

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Kan extensions along
discrete opfibrations

For a graph
Q
we denote by
\mathcal{Q}=L(Q)
the free category generated by
Q
and for a graph map
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
by
F=L(f):\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
the induced functor between free categories, as in Section
3.1
.

Recall that for
an insertion
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
the associated functor
F:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a discrete opfibration by Lemma
3.4
.
The left Kan extension
\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B:\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
of a functor
B:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
into a cocomplete category
\mathcal{C}
is then given by Perrone and Tholen
[
PT
, Corollary 5.8]
. We specialise
[
PT
, Corollary 5.8]
, which holds more generally for Kan extensions along
opfibrations between small categories, to the case at hand and give a direct proof for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
Q,Q^{\prime}
be graded
graphs,
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
an insertion and
\mathcal{C}
a cocomplete category.

The left Kan extension of a functor
B:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
along
F:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is given on
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B(v^{\prime})=\textstyle\coprod_{v\in f^{-1}(v^{\prime})}B(v),

and the components of the natural transformation
\eta:B\Rightarrow(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B)F
are
the inclusions for the coproduct.
On morphisms
q^{\prime}:v^{\prime}\to w^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}

the functor
\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B
is given by the commuting diagram

\textstyle{\coprod_{v\in f^{-1}(v^{\prime})}B(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B(q^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\coprod_{w\in f^{-1}(w^{\prime})}B(w)}
\textstyle{B(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{v}}
\scriptstyle{B(q_{v})}
\textstyle{B(w),\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{w}}

where
q_{v}:v\to w
is the unique morphism starting in
v\in f^{-1}(v^{\prime})
with
F(q_{v})=q^{\prime}
.

Proof.

To show that
(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B,\eta)
has the universal property of the left Kan extension, let
G:\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
be a functor and
\gamma:B\Rightarrow GF
a natural transformation. Any natural transformation
\delta:\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B\Rightarrow G
with
(\delta F)\circ\eta=\gamma
is determined uniquely by that condition, as
\eta
is given by the inclusions for the coproduct. To show existence, it is sufficient to verify the naturality condition, which holds, because the following diagram commutes for any morphism
q^{\prime}:v^{\prime}\to w^{\prime}

\textstyle{\coprod_{v\in f^{-1}(v^{\prime})}B(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B(q^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{v^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\coprod_{w\in f^{-1}(w^{\prime})}B(w)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{w^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{B(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{v}}
\scriptstyle{B(q_{v})}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{v}}
\textstyle{B(w)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{w}}
\textstyle{G(v^{\prime})=GF(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G(q^{\prime})}
\textstyle{GF(w)=G(w^{\prime}).}

The upper quadrilateral commutes by definition of
\mathrm{Lan}_{F}B
on the morphisms, the lower one by naturality of
\gamma
and the two triangles due to the condition
(\delta F)\circ\eta=\gamma
.
∎

A.2
Results used in the proof of Theorem
6.1

In this section, we collect some results on ends and limits of functors into a bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{V}
and a result on pushouts of such functors and natural transformations for the proof of Theorem
6.1
. The main case of interest is
\mathbf{V}=\mathrm{Grpd}
.
Throughout this section, let
\mathcal{Q}
be a small category and
\mathbf{V}
a bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category. Note that this implies that
\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{V})
is bicomplete.

Lemma A.2
.

For all functors
D:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbf{V}
the functor

\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}(-,D):\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{V})^{op}\to\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{V})

preserves limits.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the fact that
\mathrm{HOM}_{V}(-,v):\mathbf{V}^{op}\to\mathbf{V}
preserves limits for all
v\in\mathrm{Ob}\mathbf{V}
.
As
\mathbf{V}
is bicomplete and
\mathcal{Q}
small, the categories
\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{V})
and
\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q}^{op}\times\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{V})\cong\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q}^{op},\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{V}))
are bicomplete as well, and their limits are pointwise.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
F:\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Q}
,
T^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{V}
and
D\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbf{V}
be functors. There is an isomorphism

\int_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(T^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),DF(x^{\prime})\big)\cong\int_{x\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}T^{\prime}(x),D(x)\big),

which is natural in
T^{\prime}
and in
D
.

Proof.

For any object
v
in
\mathbf{V}
, we have the following bijections, which are natural in
v
:

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\left(v,\int_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(T^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),DF(x^{\prime})\big)\right)\cong\int_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\left(v,\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(T^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),DF(x^{\prime})\big)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\big(v\otimes_{\mathbf{V}}T^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),DF(x^{\prime})\big)\cong\int_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\left(T^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(v,DF(x^{\prime})\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\left(T^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),F^{*}\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(v,D(-)\big)(x^{\prime})\right)\cong\int_{x\in\mathcal{Q}}\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\left(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}T^{\prime}(x),\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(v,D(x)\big)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{x\in\mathcal{Q}}\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\left(v,\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}T^{\prime}(x),D(x)\big)\right)\cong\hom_{\mathbf{V}}\left(v,\int_{x\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}\big(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}T^{\prime}(x),D(x)\big)\right).

Here, the first identity follows from the fact that
\hom_{\mathbf{V}}(v,-)\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathrm{Set}
preserves limits, the second and third from the fact that
\mathbf{V}
is symmetric monoidal closed. The equality in the fourth step is the definition of
F^{*}
. The fifth step follows from the universal property of left Kan extension, together with the fact that for all functors
M,L\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
the set of natural transformations
M\Rightarrow L
is in bijection with
\int_{x\in\mathcal{Q}}\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M(x),L(x))
. Finally we reverse the initial three steps. The claim then follows by the Yoneda lemma.
∎

Remark A.4
.

If
\mathbf{V}=\mathrm{Grpd}
, then
\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}(A,B)
is the groupoid of functors
A\to B
, and natural transformations between them. In this case we have

\int_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mathrm{GRPd}\big(T^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),F^{*}D(x^{\prime})\big)\cong\mathrm{Nat}(T^{\prime},F^{*}D)\cong\mathrm{Nat}(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}T^{\prime},D)\cong\int_{x\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathrm{GRPd}\big(\mathrm{Lan}_{F}T^{\prime}(x),D(x)\big),

and these bijections preserve the composition of natural transformations.

We now consider the following diagrams of categories, functors and natural transformations, where
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
are cocomplete small categories and
\mathbf{V}
a bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category

(183)

T_{012}=\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}}T_{01}\!\!\!\!\coprod_{\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{1}}T_{1}}\!\!\!\!\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{12}}T_{12}:\mathcal{Q}_{012}\to\mathbf{V},

and we abbreviate
[-,-]=\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}(-,-)
and
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{Q}}[T,D]:=\int_{v\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathrm{HOM}_{\mathbf{V}}(T(v),D(v))
.

Proof.

1. Via the universal property of
\mathrm{Lan}_{F_{0}}T_{0}:\mathcal{Q}_{01}\to\mathbf{V}
the
natural transformation
\tau_{0}:T_{0}\Rightarrow T_{01}F_{0}
induces a natural transformation
\hat{\tau}_{0}:\mathrm{Lan}_{F_{0}}T_{0}\Rightarrow T_{01}
. Taking its image under
\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}}:\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q}_{01},\mathbf{V})\to\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q}_{012},\mathbf{V})
and pre-composing with the natural isomorphism
\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}F_{0}}T_{0}\cong\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}}(\mathrm{Lan}_{F_{0}}T_{0})
induced by the universal property of the Kan extension
yields a natural transformation

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0}:\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{0}}T_{0}=\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}F_{0}}T_{0}\cong\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}}(\mathrm{Lan}_{F_{0}}T_{0})\xRightarrow{\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}}\hat{\tau}_{0}}\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}}T_{01}.

(184)

We analogously define natural transformations

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}:\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{1}}T_{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{01}}T_{01},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\prime}:\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{1}}T_{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{12}}T_{12},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}:\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{2}}T_{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{12}}T_{12}.

(185)

The pushout of

and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{1}:\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{1}}T_{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{12}}T_{12}
in
\mathrm{CAT}(\mathcal{Q}_{012},\mathbf{V})

(189)

3. We apply the natural isomorphism from Lemma
A.3
to all entries in diagram (
189
) except the bottom one.
For the top left entry of (
189
) this yields the following isomorphism in
\mathbf{V}

\displaystyle\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{Q}_{012}}[\mathrm{Lan}_{J_{0}}T_{0},D_{012}]\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}[T_{0},D_{012}{J_{0}}]=\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}[T_{0},D_{0}]

where we used in the last step that
D_{012}J_{0}=D_{0}
by (
182
). For the other entries, this is analogous.
Composing the morphisms on the arrows with these isomorphisms then
gives the diagram in the theorem.
∎
